# MOR Findings Log, continuation: review round 2

*Continues MOR-findings-log.md from F53. Kept as a separate file during the round 2 work so each decision is a small write; merged into the main log at the consolidated revision.*

**Names, old to new (the rename pass, 10 October 2026, `docs/rename-pass.md`).** The entries below, and every other record, keep the names of their day; read them with this map. Wording only: no rule and nothing on the wire changed.
- **Layers** (F212): Envelope → **Envelopes**; Finance → **Money**; Law → **Agreements**; Production → **Development**; Identity and Text unchanged. "Finance rule 15" in a record is Money rule 15; `MIP-law-draft-10.md` is `MIP-agreements-draft-10.md`. In code: `layers::FINANCE`, `LAW`, `PRODUCTION`, `ENVELOPE_AND_TEXT` are `layers::MONEY`, `AGREEMENTS`, `DEVELOPMENT`, `ENVELOPES_AND_TEXT`, numbers unchanged (2, 3, 4, 1); the modules `law`, `finance`, `envelope` are `agreements`, `money`, `envelopes`.
- **The core's texts** (F269, the MIR rename pass, 12 October 2026, `docs/mir-rename-pass.md`): one core text is a **MIR**, the six together the **MICS**. "The Money MIP" in a record is the Money MIR; "the MIPs", meaning the core, is the MICS; `spec/MIP-agreements-draft-11.md` is `spec/MIR-agreements-draft-11.md`, and `spec/02-MOR-core-v22.md` is `spec/02-MOR-MICS-v22.md` (pointers are left at the old names). In code: `MIPS` is `MIRS`, `agreements::Mips` is `Mirs`, `with_mips` is `with_mirs`. cMIPs keep their name; MIP now means only a proposal toward MOR 2.
- **Keys** ("The two keys renamed", 5 October 2026): the safety key → **the chain key**; the everyday key → **the signing key**; so a safety commitment, seed or scheme is a chain-key commitment, seed or scheme. "Everyday act", "everyday check" and "everyday line" are unchanged (acts signed with the signing key). The hash tag `"MOR/safety"` is unchanged; `module-airgap-safety-signer-draft-4.md` and the safety seed Modules are `module-airgap-chain-key-signer-draft-4.md`, `module-chain-key-seed-words-draft-1.md` and `module-chain-key-seed-hex-draft-1.md`.

## Round 2 review patterns (working rules)

Each finding names its pattern before its fix. Round 2 named three; the reaction added a fourth.

1. **What decides is signed by the wrong party.** A record that decides status or money counts only alongside something the party who benefits cannot produce alone: a second signature from the other side, or a valid act it points to.
2. **A lower layer depends on a higher layer's data.** A layer's validity may depend only on data that layer's own clients hold. Push a small field down; never pull a rule up.
3. **A rule that protects the owner also traps the owner.** Every protection an owner chooses must be removable by the owner's safety key alone; every protection others hold over a party must need that party's signature to change.
4. **A rule rests on a fact nobody can check.** Make the fact an act someone signs, move the rule to client conformance and say so, or drop it.

## Working rule: the test apps keep the layers apart, and talk through MOR (Nobody, allegedly, 17 October 2026)

"We attempt to keep the layers separate and force information to travel between apps via MOR." For the apps built for the tests and demonstrations: each app keeps to its layer, and whatever one app prepares for another travels as acts on MOR (sealed to oneself where it is private), never through a shared file or a side channel, even where that is absurd as UX ("The absurd was more about Dub not signing a tip"). Roughly: Dub, Text and Envelopes (talking); Damgar, Money; Edubba, Agreements and collectives (with agents and releases); Kišib, the Identity layer's emergencies (chain keys). The apps show how MOR runs by making its record the only road between them. *A later product may fold them together; these are instruments.*

## Working rule: options with a forced default (Nobody, allegedly, F59)

Where the core offers the owner several options, one of them is the default when none is selected. The default is one of the declared options, never a third, weaker rule. This may be repeated elsewhere in the protocol when needed.

## Working rule: core business, or the participants' choice (Nobody, allegedly, F265)

Before any question is put: "Is this core business? Or has core defined how the choice is made by participants?" Where the core already gives participants the means to choose (their terms, their split plan, their vows, their cMIPs), the answer is theirs, and the question is not put. The core keeps only what no choice may break.

## F370. Three texts: a fresh chain-key seed as the rule, a transfer to oneself naming the vault, and an audio Module (21 October 2026; F361, F362, F355 (c); Dubsar Suite)

**Written by a Dubsar Suite session** (`docs/texts-r0-report.md`), on decisions already taken; each draft for Nobody, allegedly, to approve.

1. **The air-gapped chain-key signer Module, draft 6** (F361, F362). Rule 3.3 makes a fresh seed the rule at every change of keys, its first key at the rotation's index (key *n* of the seed drawn for the rotation at position *n*, as Kišib builds it). Its backup is shown, part of it asked back, and only then is the rotation signed. Rule 3.7 puts the two backups in that order: the chain-key seed before anything commits to it, the signing seed once the act installing it counts. **No exception (decided by Nobody, allegedly, 21 October 2026):** "No, it has been decided that the core operates this way, you cannot change the core. At each rotation, two set of keys are created, always." The session had first kept deriving from the current seed as an exception chosen on the device; it is removed, and the device never derives the next key from the current seed, whoever asks. The seed Modules get a draft 2 each, because one sentence was now wrong ("derives every chain key of an identity"); backups and keys are unchanged. *To build:* the Module's code (`modules/airgap/`) still implements draft 5's rule 3.3, a fresh seed as a choice, with no confirmation before signing.
2. **The payment cMIP, draft 4** (F355 (c)). Where `fulfils` is the very act `paid-to` names as declaring the vault, the payment is a transfer to oneself: payer and payee one identity, no purchase, or invalid (step 4a). It owes nothing and is shown as a transfer to one's vault; draft 3's reading (one's own pointer) stays valid. **For the MOR lead:** Money lists what a receipt's field 5 and a claim's field 4 name (obligation, agreement, offer, payee pointer); whether that list is closed is asked in the report, section 3. Nothing in the core was changed. *To build:* step 4a in `cmips/payment`, and Damgar naming the vault declaration.
3. **The audio Module, draft 1, with its code** (decided by Nobody, allegedly, 21 October 2026: "let's have an audio module", M4A). One M4A file: the MP4 container with exactly one AAC-LC sound track, mono or stereo, and no picture, text or chapter track; the video Module's checks, refusals and stripping, said in plain words; limits 64 MiB less 16 bytes, two hours, 48,000 Hz (two hours, the session's lean, "Agreed" by Nobody, allegedly, 21 October 2026). Code `modules/audio/`, which reads with the video Module's own code. The video Module accepts and refuses exactly what it did: its vectors are unchanged byte for byte, and the part of it the display client bundles is byte for byte as released. No app uses it yet.

The three new texts carry the house prefix (F356): Dubsar Air-Gapped Chain-Key Signer, Dubsar Payments, Dubsar Audio; the seed Modules Dubsar Words and Dubsar Hex.

The core states only what a verifier checks of what it is handed (an inclusion or consistency proof's content, as Identity's checks read it) in a fixed encoding the core gives; how a client asks a home, and how it finds deliveries, leave the core's open list for the relay transport cMIP.
2. **The unit specification (3) and the signature-scheme specification (8, 34):** each written as a Development kind with its fields, since Money's and Identity's rules name them by hash and a verifier decodes them. The CDDL conventions stated once.

5. **A MIR's spec hash (17):** one rule, stated once, matching what the code computes; Identity's "the hash of the frozen text" reworded to it.
6. **The final line break (12):** a frozen text may end with exactly one; Development's root check allows it.
7. **Unicode (2):** pinned to the version the core's tables pin today, as a number, with no "as of".
8. **X-Wing's hash (54):** written into Envelopes at the freeze; a step of the freeze procedure.

## F367. S1's remaining verdict questions (21 October 2026; questions 4, 5, 9, 22, 37, 44, 45, 49, 51)

**Taken by the project lead under delegation**, each read in the freeze-ready texts:

1. **An act under a pending rotation (4):** pending, never valid, until the rotation counts (Identity rule 28 says so; everyday check 2 is worded to match: a binding to a pending rotation makes the act pending, not invalid).
2. **A home's dishonesty (5):** as Identity rule 45 says, not as the session leaned: the conflict survives the home's own rotations, and, where the identity requires audit, both receipts sit under cosigned log summaries (for such an identity, step 2 already ends the conflict when only one is logged). "Conflicting receipts" step 3 is aligned to it; rule 44 says "pass receipt check 1", as step 1 does, since a receipt in conflict counts for nothing.
3. **Text rule 14 at a Text-only verifier (9):** unknown for a text carrying one object of either allowed shape, invalid for any other `objects`.
4. **A collective's pointer left outside a fork (22):** it counts for payments made before the fork's line, and not after.
5. **A malformed specification against "invalid beats unknown" (37, second half):** an act naming one is unknown, unless a rule the verifier can apply shows it invalid.
6. **"Only acts valid in their own format count" (44):** it reaches acts of the core's types; a cMIP's record counts where the verifier reads its format; transfer field 3's bound is a status rule, not a format rule.
7. **Agreements' contradictions (45):** an abandonment outcome where a holder has a named stake successor: the stake passes by succession; genesis and rotations lie outside any area; task 14 is judicial, as Development's table says; a fork's completeness checks non-member holders' chain signatures; take-ups listed in record field 3; terms fields 26 and 27 placed in the tiers table.
8. **A clone placing a successor elsewhere than the dead member's place (49):** invalid when marked with the plan alone, as a clone that reorders the other parties.
9. **Money's dependence on Development (51):** stated, not removed: a Money client decodes Development's specification format (a rail Module's fields 5, 11 and 13, and whether a specification is one) and nothing more of Development. Money's "never depends" sentence narrows to say so.

**Where:** the next pass on the freeze-ready texts.

## F366. Collectives' member keys across Money and Agreements (21 October 2026; S1's questions 20, 43, 10)

**Taken by the project lead under delegation** (Nobody, allegedly, 21 October 2026: "you'll decide the rest"), on his principle of F358: "It is a money matter and money areas should be able to do it. A founder should not have a special position if the constitution does not say so."

1. **Every Money act, not pointers alone (question 20).** Money rule 7 lists every Money act a member key may sign for its collective (payee pointer, receipt, claim, obligation, creditor's release), each judged by Agreements as scoped, under the area that reaches Money and that area's holders and threshold; a Money-only client shows them unknown, as it does today for pointers. A split service's grant is judged under the area that issued it (rule 74). *Cost:* a Money-only client shows more of a collective's acts unknown.
2. **When a member key counts, and when it stops (question 43).** It counts within the areas in force for the act it signs, from the moment the founding or clone naming it is in force, until a later version names another key for that holder or the holder leaves. The member's own change of keys leaves it as it is (F353). A rotation of the member that disowns the very signature that named the key is judged as any member's signature the collective's line placed: it stays valid for the collective, as "Made before, made after" says. A grant to oneself is accepted by the grantor's own type 1 acceptance (F351).
3. **Text rule 14's two cases (question 10).** Where the signing key is a grant key, a member key included, the grant-key case decides.

**Where:** the next pass on the freeze-ready texts (Money 11 rule 7; Agreements 17 rules 60, 73, 74, 80; Text 11 rule 14).

## F365. What an act under a revoked grant, shown disputed, confers (21 October 2026; S1's question 24)

**Taken by the project lead under delegation**, following F359 (decided this morning: "visible to everyone, never erased"). As for an act a rotation disowned: an act under a revoked grant that F359 shows disputed **confers nothing on an agreement chain** (no stake, no obligation, no consent: Agreements rule 6's sentence for a rotation) and is **shown**; Money treats it as it treats a disputed act after a change of keys. **The grantor may still adopt it** (rule 76: acknowledging, citing or paying on it), and then it binds. Every passage that says "void" for an act a revocation does not hold (the tie rule, rule 79, the scenarios) says "void, or disputed under rule 76".

**Why:** binding the grantor (the session's option b) would let a thief with a lane key and an accomplice who acknowledges sign the victim into a debt: what decides would be signed by the wrong party (pattern 1). Shown, not binding, keeps both sides visible; the honest grantor who meant it adopts it. *Cost:* a payee who relied on a revoked app's promise (a debt to come, not a payment made) holds a visible dispute, not a debt, unless the grantor adopts it. A payment already made stays made on its rail; its receipt is the payee's own act and stands.

## F364. Keepers, the remaining details (21 October 2026; S1's questions 7, 25, 40, 41, 42)

**Delegated by Nobody, allegedly, 21 October 2026:** "Let's do this… I'll contribute where I can, you'll decide the rest." **Taken by the project lead**, each read in Agreements 17 and Identity 15 first; none widens what a keeper may do.

1. **An anchor shows a deadline met, not missed (question 41).** F358's words: "a deadline is shown missed by an anchor, of the act itself or of a keeper's sequence". An anchor shows only that something existed by a point, never that it did not exist before. So: an anchor of the act itself shows the act made in time (a deadline met); a deadline missed is shown only by a keeper's anchored sequence that holds no record of the act up to a point past the deadline; a keeper's word alone never says late (F358 unchanged there). *Not chosen:* an act proving its own lateness by naming something that exists only after the deadline (a real proof, but a new mechanism). **Confirmed by Nobody, allegedly, the same afternoon: "Yes, it does."**
2. **A keeper's own later rotation (question 7).** As decided in round 2's Q2 (G2, "Agreed"), not as the session leaned: a keeper's record that a rule reads to place an act, or that a relied-on version's completeness rests on, keeps the effect the rule gave it whatever the keeper's later rotation disowned, shown as a dispute ("a witness chosen in advance cannot un-witness"). Agreements 17 states it; Identity's rule that evidence counts only while its signer's chain keeps it gains this as its stated exception, in both texts' words alike. The session's lean narrowed it to a collective's line; that would have undone a decided rule.
3. **Several keepers (question 40).** Each keeper's record is judged alone, against that keeper's own record of the rotation; the agreement's rule (any one, a threshold, all) counts how many keepers' records stand before their own record of it.
4. **One sequence per keeper (question 25).** A keeper keeps one sequence for its records (a MUST for keepers); records of two sequences are never ordered against each other, so a keeper's second sequence protects nothing against the first. The other details as S1 wrote them: one record per act, public, chained by `prev`, its named signer matching the act's.
5. **A deal with no keeper (question 42).** A declaration's line decides only what no relied-on version holds: what others relied on stays complete (rule 47) before any declaration's line.

**Where:** the next pass on the freeze-ready texts (Agreements 17's keepers section and rule 33, Identity 15's evidence rule).

## F363. A keeper counts for every act that belongs to its agreement, however the act links to it (21 October 2026; S1's question 39)

**Found by the freeze-ready texts session** (S1, question 39): Agreements rule 42 counts a keeper record only where "the keeper is one named by an agreement the act's `objects` names". Many acts that belong to a deal link to it otherwise: a receipt names its claim (field 9), a claim names its agreement in its own field. Their records could never count, though the texts rely on them; and rule 7's "a keeper that recorded both" two work claims could never be met.

**Walked back through the history first** (asked by Nobody, allegedly: "Deserves a big slow down… Let's do some archeology"): keepers were born as notaries the parties choose when they sign, recording "the deal and the acts around it", so a change of keys cannot erase what was relied on (the early core; F11's story); sealed (F37); guarded by F58 against a thief buying "disputed" from any keeper, by requiring the keeper to be named by the act's own agreement. F58 wrote "the act's own agreement" as "the agreement its `objects` names", true then of every act on a deal; receipts and claims came to link otherwise later. So the question was not how far keepers reach, but whether the wording still said what they always were.

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly, 21 October 2026:** "Yes, it was always part of the agreement, just not directly stated into it. Makes sense to rework it to ensure all participants to the agreement can fall under the bracket." (a), the project lead's lean: a keeper record counts for an act that **belongs to** an agreement naming that keeper; an act belongs to an agreement when it names it in `objects`, or through the act it answers (a receipt through the claim it names, a claim or payment through its agreement field), so every participant's acts on the agreement, payer's and payee's alike, fall under its keepers. The keeper is still one the parties chose. An act belonging to no agreement (a post, a work claim alone, a rotation) has no keeper of its own: only an anchor places it. Rule 7: conflicting work claims are decided by anchoring, or by a keeper of an agreement both claims belong to.

**Not chosen:** (b) any keeper named by any agreement the signer is party to (a keeper chosen for one deal speaking for acts outside it; the history gives no reason); (c) as written. **Shelved, not proposed:** that an anchored keeper sequence might count as clock evidence for anyone; it would change what keepers are, not for before the freeze.

**Cost:** a verifier may follow two acts, not one, to see whether an act belongs to an agreement; a client judging deals implements Agreements already (F11).

**Where:** Agreements draft 17, rules 42 and 7 (and the definition of "belongs to"), in the next pass on the freeze-ready texts; the core's check and a verdict vector (a receipt recorded by its deal's keeper before the payee's rotation: disputed; the same recorded by an unnamed keeper: void).

## F362. Every signing path checks the key first; a change of keys makes two new papers (21 October 2026; H80, H79; Dubsar Suite)

**Found in the walkthrough** (H80, sitting 5): after a change of keys in Kišib, Edubba signed a reliance with the retired key and said it was signed; a correction was refused, with nowhere to give the new words. **The cause:** `Desk.witness` signed without `keyCurrent`, the check F335 asks for "before every act". The audit of every app found the same gap wider: in Edubba, every path of `real.ts` (a founding, the founder's sealed shares, a member change, the money proposal and its record, the payee pointer, a keeper's record, a registered resignation), of `publishing.ts` (a work or vow as oneself, a sale contested or confirmed, a key delivered) and of `money.ts` (a payment or an anchor prepared for Damgar), and the real collective's own key held in Edubba; in Dub, a vow's sale contested and the key delivery that runs by itself on every refresh; in Damgar, a promise of a collective (the member's own key asks the other holders). Ummia checked everywhere. **Fixed:** each checks first and refuses in its app's own words, nothing signed or sent; Edubba's check reads the identity's own homes too and refuses on a contested chain; the key delivery shared by Dub and Edubba takes the app's check as a required argument; where Edubba says a key changed, the box for the new words is right there. Paths signing with another key are unaffected: a member's own key for a collective, a grant (lane) key, a new identity's key, a share, a one-time key.

**What became of the reliance:** homes and relays store it (a home checks the signature against the key its own binding set, retired or not); every verifier calls it void (Identity rules 34 and 35). **No core finding:** no verdict changes. *A question for the relay transport cMIP (the Suite lead's since the leads were corrected):* whether a home should refuse an act signed under a binding a counting rotation replaced, beyond the kept tips; to weigh against late acts that are inside the kept ancestry.

**H79, built (F361):** at every change of keys, at an escape, at a vault change and at a succession named in a rotation, Kišib makes a fresh seed and commits its key *n* (the rotation's position, the air-gapped Module's key index "even across a fresh seed"); the old paper is refused as spent once the change counts, said plainly. A collective's rotation already dealt new shares from a fresh seed. *When each set of words is shown, decided by Nobody, allegedly, 21 October 2026:* "From a safety point of view, confirming the new chain key comes before signing the rotation, confirming the new signing key can come after." So the new chain key's words are written and three typed back before the rotation is signed (as the air-gapped Module's rule 3.3 reads), the new signing key's after the homes' receipts. Until the change counts, the old paper only sends that same change again (Kišib signs nothing); Kišib closed before the change was sent signed nothing, and the unused paper is worthless. *Why it was asked:* shown after the receipts, a Kišib closed between sending and writing would have lost the new paper, and with it every later change of keys.

**Found while testing, for the Suite lead:** after a member's change of keys, Kišib could not tie two private acts at the member's homes (one Edubba's, one Damgar's) to the cards those apps show, whose latest acts never reached the homes; left out, they took the member's own key for a collective with them. `docs/h80-report.md`, question 2.

## F361. A change of keys makes fresh chain-key paper (21 October 2026; H79 reopened)

**Found by the Dubsar Suite lead** (`claude/MOR-suite-for-the-mor-lead.md` in the project, item 1): Kišib reused the same paper for every change of keys, on a reading in its README marked "to be confirmed" and never confirmed; H79 was closed as designed on that reading. **Decided by Nobody, allegedly, 21 October 2026:** "No, it does not stay the same. Unsure why sessions believe so. A rotation generates two new sets of keys, chain and signing."

**Judged by the MOR lead:** no MIR changes. Identity commits only the hash of the next chain key; where it comes from is a client's and a key Module's. The air-gapped chain-key signer Module (draft 5, rule 3.3) has it the other way round: the next key from the current seed, a fresh seed only "if the current one may be exposed". Its next draft makes a fresh seed the rule at every change of keys, with both new sets of words shown and backed up (rule 3.7), and the seed Modules' notes follow. *Where:* the Dubsar Suite lead's (cMIPs and Modules are the suite's, Nobody, allegedly, 21 October 2026), with Kišib's fresh seed (the H80 session).

Where a reason helps a reader understand or check a rule, it stays as commentary in the text's own words. ## F359. An act under a revoked grant that another acknowledged is disputed, not void (21 October 2026; F352 (D))

**Pattern:** 1 and 3. Agreements rule 76 (the tie rule) voids every act under a grant that its revocation's history does not hold. Since F352 a revocation must cite each act under the lane, and an act it misses is void, even where the counterparty signed a receipt for it. The grantor's list, alone, decided what another identity had been given in good faith.

**Put by the project lead (F352's open (D)), decided by Nobody, allegedly, 21 October 2026 ("Agreed"):** as for a change of keys (F57), an act under a revoked grant that the revocation's history does not hold, but that another identity acknowledged, is shown **disputed** rather than void. An act nobody else acknowledged stays void, so a revocation still cuts off a stolen or misused grant cleanly. *The project lead's lean.*

**Stated cost:** a thief holding a grant key, with an accomplice who acknowledges the act, gets "disputed" rather than "void": visible to everyone, never erased. The same cost F57 already accepts for a change of keys.

**Why:** no grantor's omission, careless or chosen, erases what a counterparty holds a signature for. A dispute shows both sides; a void shows one.

**Where the gaps actually are** (walked with Nobody, allegedly, the same morning; "Agreed"): a lane's acts form a chain, each naming the one before, so an honest grantor's app holding the latest act can reach every earlier one. It misses only the tail: an act the app made but had not yet stored at the grantor's places (offline, crashed), or one made at the moment of the revocation, neither citing the other (a race). A gap in the middle comes only from a revocation written to leave an act out on purpose. So the honest case F359 protects is a payee in a race; the case it blocks is a deliberate omission; and the stated cost sits in the same race: a stolen grant key's last acts before the revocation, acknowledged by an accomplice, show disputed, bounded by the grant's own limits (cap per act, expiry). *The project lead's first framing, that a careless app could drop any act, overstated it: careless means only the tail.*

**Where:** the freeze-ready drafts (Agreements rule 76 and the grant section, worded beside F57's rule in Identity), the core's check, and a verdict vector (an acknowledged act missed by a revocation: disputed; an unacknowledged one: void). F352's tests stand: a payee holding only its own act finds it counted when cited. **A keeper's record too (decided by Nobody, allegedly, the same morning: "Yes agreed", the project lead's lean):** as F57 allows for a rotation, an act under a grant that a named keeper recorded before recording the revocation is shown disputed rather than void, judged as rule 42 judges any record (the verifier holds the act, the act is valid, the keeper is named by an agreement the act names). Few lane acts name such an agreement, so the path matters mainly for acts under a grant made inside a deal that names keepers. The drafts write both rescues once, for a rotation and a revocation alike.

## F358. Round 3's questions: the collective's payee pointer, a lone seller's pointer, and keepers redefined (19 October 2026)

**1. Who signs a collective's payee pointer.** Built: the collective's own key, in the founder's Edubba, since Money reads only pointers signed under Identity's binding and a member key is a grant key (scoped). **Decided by Nobody, allegedly, (b): "It is a money matter and money areas should be able to do it. A founder should not have a special position if the constitution does not say so."** Money draft 10 extends rule 7 (F350) to payee pointers (type 0): a pointer signed with a member key the founding or clone names for an area reaching Money counts, scoped, judged by Agreements with that area's holders and threshold. Edubba signs the collective's pointer with the money area's holders' keys, gathered as any act of that area. Built in round 3. *Cost:* a Money-only client shows such a pointer unknown and cannot pay through it. My lean was (a); withdrawn, the same principle as F353.

**2. A lone seller's deal naming a split service. Decided ("Agreed"), (a):** a sale under that deal pays the service's own pointer, the commitment's payee still the seller and its "paid to" naming the service's pointer, as field 14 makes the service the agreement's designated pointer; the seller's own pointer stays theirs, for tips. *Cost:* a reader follows field 14 to see why the payment counts.

**3 and 4. Keepers, redefined (Nobody, allegedly: "sounds like they need to be redefined"; "Ok, agreed").** Round 3 found a keeper alone cannot show a deadline missed (rule 33): its sequence carries order, not time; and that the keeper record (type 2) is decided in text (OF21) but has no format in the core, while verdicts rest on it. Walked through: keepers were born to place acts before a rotation (theft recovery cannot erase what was relied on), then took on placing a collective's acts against its lines (C4), relied-on completeness (F314), fork evidence, and deadlines (I7, rule 33): all order, except deadlines, which are time. **Decided:** a keeper is a signed ordering mechanism serving proofs: it signs a sequence (this act, then this, then this rotation), checkable from any copy; its record format goes into the core (OF21's `{ 0 => [act, signer] }`, type 2 read by the core). Only anchors place anything on the clock. A keeper may anchor its own sequence, placing everything recorded up to it; anchoring is optional, and serious keepers will; an agreement needing deadlines may require it in its terms. Rule 33 becomes: a deadline is shown missed by an anchor, of the act itself or of a keeper's sequence; a keeper's word alone never says late. The remaining trust, that a keeper records as acts arrive, is rule 42's, stated. **Where:** the freeze-ready drafts (Agreements' keepers section, rule 33, type 2's format, the core's dispatcher). Round 3 leaves the keeper deadline path unbuilt and its keeper logs client-stated until then.

## F357. Round 3: a payment request to a split service, and confirming a vow sale from Edubba (19 October 2026)

**1. Found by round 3:** a buyer's invoice request (money messages type 3) is sealed to the payee; for a collective with a split service, the service must answer (Agreements rule 16; field 14's grantee is the designated pointer and receipt signer), but it cannot open what is sealed to the collective without the collective's encryption key, kept in the founder's Edubba. The money messages cMIP (draft 3) is silent. **Decided ("Agreed"), (a):** money messages draft 4: where the payee's agreement in force names a split service (field 14), the payer sends the invoice request to the service, sealed to the service's own encryption key, the collective still the payee in the commitment; the service answers with an invoice from its node and later receipts under the collective's grant key. The payer's Damgar reads field 14 as its pointer check already reads Agreements. Built in round 3. Declined: (b) the service given the collective's encryption key (it could read everything sealed to the collective, key deliveries included); (c) a member's app forwarding requests (payments waiting for a member to be online). Transport, not core: no verdict changes.

**2. Confirming a vow sale from Edubba. Decided ("Agreed"), (a):** Edubba prepares the confirmation, sealed to oneself, and Damgar signs it, as Dub's tips are prepared; contesting stays in Edubba and Dub. Replaces what was built, (c), where Edubba only pointed to Dub or Damgar for confirming. Why: the working rule that what one app prepares for another travels as acts sealed to oneself; confirming and contesting side by side where the vow is seen.

## F356. The cMIPs branded, "Dubsar" as the house (19 October 2026)

**Asked by Nobody, allegedly:** "All our cMIPs have very generic names, they should be branded to avoid confusion." A generic name ("the payment cMIP") reads as the standard, against the competitive layer; a cMIP's identity is its specification hash, its name only a label.

**Decided ("Yes, agreed"):** (a) one house prefix for every cMIP and Module written in this repository: "Dubsar" (Dubsar Payments, Dubsar Grant Limits, Dubsar Relay Transport, and so on), the name of the site and the homes, never the protocol's. Another author's would carry their own house. Done in the freeze-ready drafts session: each text's title and the apps' wording; the findings log keeps the old names as history. Declined: (b) a coined name per cMIP, as many names to invent and remember.

## F355. A transfer to one's vault, from Damgar (19 October 2026)

**Asked (H46):** "Damgar should offer a 'transfer to vault' option." Round 3 found the payment cMIP (draft 3) already carries it: payee and payer both oneself, paid-to the vault (`[1, declared-by, entry]`), on-chain to the vault key adjusted by the commitment. Fulfils naming one's own payee pointer in force is a reading, not stated.

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly ("Agreed"):** (a) as every Damgar payment, no new act: Damgar prepares the transfer, shows the vault, the amount and what it can say of the fee (the wallet shows the exact fee before the person confirms); the person pays in their wallet (on regtest, the test wallet) and pastes the proof back; Damgar checks it and keeps the record, commitment and salt included, sealed to them at their homes, saying plainly that this money is spent only with the chain key and that record. The pointer reading stands until (c): the payment cMIP's next draft lets fulfils name the vault declaration for a transfer to oneself, built after (a)'s screens. Declined: (b), Damgar sending by itself through a spending permission, which would reverse F338 (no wallet connect) and F351 (no permission reading the balance) for one rare action.

**Why:** a transfer to oneself has no counterparty; nothing in it changes a verdict for anyone else, so it stays in the existing payment path. One paste suits a rare, deliberate act.

## F353. A collective's members act with their own keys, granted by the founding itself (18 October 2026)

**Asked by Nobody, allegedly**, from the web Edubba's question on money ("the signee who have the right to act on money can do so for the collective in the other apps; the question is how"), then the second signature, then: "let's try to solve it now, there is an opportunity hiding somewhere near". Today a collective's acts need its one signing key, held by the founder's Edubba (Agreements rule 59: one holder, a threshold, or a custodian); Ummia round 1 and Damgar round 2 met the same wall.

**Nobody, allegedly:** "I think we need to rethink what 'collective keys are'… it is a single key, but it does not need to be, because the ID is a hash… Each member has its own so the collective chain still knows who did what." *The project lead* laid out (1) the identity binding several signing keys (an Identity change, reopened before the freeze) and (2) grant keys, already core ("like a device of the collective limited to that scope", F128). **Nobody, allegedly:** "2 works because it is exactly that, the collective granting powers to the members, but it should not require an extra step."

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly, 18 October 2026 ("Agreed"):** each member acts for the collective with a key of their own: the founding names, for each holder of an area, a key their app makes when they sign the founding (as it makes the one-time key for their share); the founding is the grant, the holder's founding signature its acceptance; a member change does the same in its clone (a new member's key named, a leaving member's dropped). Each act shows which member signed; an area needing two signatures takes one member's act and another member's signature; the collective's own signing key is needed only for rare constitutional acts, and may itself be held as shares (rule 59's threshold) so no one holds it day to day. *Suggested by the project lead on Nobody, allegedly's idea.*

**Where the key is named, corrected** (found by the round 3 session, 19 October 2026: the founding cannot name a key made when it is signed, since everyone signs the same text and the key would arrive too late; the same at every clone). **Decided by Nobody, allegedly ("Right… It's ok as a cost"), on the project lead's correction:** the founding names the areas and their holders (identities), never keys; **each holder's own signature of the founding (or clone) carries the public half of their key**, made by their app at that moment; the founding is the grant, the signature its naming and acceptance; the key backs nothing until the founding (or clone) is complete. *Stated cost:* if someone never signs, the keys already made are left unused. The private half never leaves the member's app (sealed to themself, below). The sentence for Agreements becomes: a holder's signature of a founding or clone may name a key; it is a grant key within the areas that founding or clone gives the holder; the founding or clone is its grant, the signature its naming and acceptance; it counts from the moment the founding or clone is complete. *The project lead's first mechanics (the key inside the founding text) were wrong.*

**How the member key reaches the member's other apps** (asked by the round 3 session, 19 October 2026: made in Dub's Signing tab or Edubba, used in Damgar, Ummia, the web Edubba): **decided by Nobody, allegedly** ("B) makes sense. Then other ways of storing and using keys can be built above, Nostr experimented with a few there are mistakes to learn from"): (b), the project lead's lean: the app that makes the key leaves it as a private act sealed to the member's own identity, as the accepted list (F340) and money drafts travel; any app holding the identity opens it; the key stays the same through the member's own change of keys (the collective unaffected), re-sealed to the new encryption key in Kišib's change of keys. *Stated cost:* the key sits encrypted at the member's homes. Not chosen: (a) rebuilt from the signing words (nothing travels; every member key changing with each change of the member's keys, a member change needed each time). Other ways of keeping and using keys are for the competitive layer.

**Core changes:** one sentence in Agreements (next draft), beside F351's: a founding or clone may name, for a holder of an area, a key; it is a grant key within that area, the founding or clone its grant, the holder's signature its acceptance. No change to Identity. Built in round 3 (Edubba founding and member changes, Dub's Signing tab, Damgar and Ummia acting for a collective).

## F352. Damgar round 2's four questions (18 October 2026)

**From** `docs/damgar-round-2-report.md`, questions 1 to 4, each decided by Nobody, allegedly, 18 October 2026, on the project lead's questions:

1. **A lane's revocation and the tie rule** (Agreements rule 76): an act under a grant counts only if the revocation's history holds it, so a revocation voids every act under the lane it does not cite, receipts for payments made included. **"Agreed":** (A) as built (the app keeps its lane acts at the identity's places; Damgar cites every act it finds and says how many) as first decided with (B), chaining Dub's lane acts so a revocation could cite only the last. **Corrected the same evening by the session's probe:** Dub's lane acts are already chained, but `prev` sits inside the lock, and each payment is sealed to its own payee, so a payee holding only its own act cannot follow the chain from the last; for it, an act not cited is void (rule 76, the ending wins: r1 NotBacked once r2 is missing). The project lead's (B) was wrong. **Decided by Nobody, allegedly ("Agreed"):** (A′), the session's lean: the chain kept, and the revocation citing every act under the lane, as built; tests that each payee holding only its own act finds it counted, and that citing only the last fails for such a payee. Not chosen: (B′) giving each payee the content keys of later acts (privacy); (C′) counting an act the revocation cannot open as held (weakens the tie rule for everyone). *Stated cost:* a public revocation lists every act's id (anyone can count the lane's payments), a long lane makes a long revocation, an act Damgar misses is voided. **Open for the freeze-ready drafts (D), the project lead's proposal:** as for a change of keys (F57), an act under a revoked grant that the revocation's history does not hold, but that another identity acknowledged (a payee's receipt), is shown disputed rather than void. Not chosen at this stage: (C) a clock's stamp on every lane act.
2. **A grant carries no words** (a lane's name travels only privately): **"Agreed":** not now. A name on a public act tells the world which apps and devices one uses; the scope and limits already say what the grant permits. *The session's and the project lead's lean.*
3. **An answer to an invoice request names no amount**, so the grant limits cMIP cannot judge a receipts lane's cap on it: **"Agreed":** the money messages cMIP's next draft puts the amount in the answer, so every act under a lane is judged by the core rather than the app's word. *The session's and the project lead's lean.* Asked with it: should the money messages cMIP be core? *The project lead, on the admission test:* no; a verifier never judges these messages, a lost one wrongs nobody, and they follow today's rails (an invoice per payment), which reusable offers would remove; unlike the textual forms (F349), better alternatives wait. **"Understood."**
4. **The sealed post's media specification**: plain text sealed as an Envelopes publication names the Text MIR's hash as its media specification. **"Confirmed".**

**Core changes:** none (the money messages cMIP draft 3; Dub's lane acts chained).

## F351. A lane is a grant to oneself; a payee's Damgar may hold a receive-only link to its node (18 October 2026)

**From** the Damgar round 2 session's questions before building.

1. **Whom a lane's grant names as grantee.** Agreements rule 73 (F128): a grant names "the grantee's own identity, which signs to accept it"; Dub has no identity of its own; F338 says a lane names a key, never a known app; the MIRs are silent on a grant whose grantee is its own grantor (the core accepts one today). **Decided by Nobody, allegedly ("Yes, agreed"), on the project lead's lean:** a grant to oneself. The person is grantor and grantee; the app makes and holds the grant key; the acceptance is the person's own (signed through Damgar); each lane is its own grant, its words naming it ("Dub on the Mac"); revocation as for any grant. One sentence written into Agreements: a grant whose grantee is its own grantor is valid, its acceptance the grantor's. Not chosen: an identity per app (identities, homes and keys multiplied); a bare-key grantee (rule 73 changed more deeply).
2. **(the session's question 3) Can the payee's Damgar answer a Lightning invoice request by itself?** Only the payee's node makes an invoice; on-chain cannot carry a small tip (below 330 satoshis refused, six blocks). **Decided by Nobody, allegedly ("Yes, my decision was an unnecessary hurdle."), on the project lead's lean:** (a) a receive-only link: the payee gives Damgar a node permission that only makes invoices (no paying, no balance, no moving money), off by default, explained in Settings, revocable, the manual path kept. Revises F338's "no wallet connect" for receiving only: Damgar still can never move money, which was the reason. *Stated cost:* whoever steals the permission can make invoices in the node's name, never take anything. Not chosen: (b) Lightning kept manual (one tap would not hold for small tips); (c) a rail with no per-payment invoice (reusable offers answered by the node; competitive layer, for the report).

3. **(the session's question 2) Who pays the reposter's referral share in round 2, with no split service?** **Decided by Nobody, allegedly ("Agreed"):** (A), the session's lean: the lone seller's offer states the share in its words (field 4); the buyer's claim names the repost (Money claim field 6, the evidence of rule 20, F75); the seller's Damgar prepares the share's payment to the reposter when it signs the receipt (under the seller's lanes, otherwise in To sign). *Stated cost:* a promise in words, not judged. *Not a gap* ("I think this was discussed in the past"; the project lead had first suggested a referral field in the standing offer, withdrawn): F215 already says a lone seller's offer divides nothing, and a percentage owed to anyone needs a plan, so an agreement, even for a simple sale. The judged form is a one-party agreement with a split plan carrying the role share, paid by the split service: round 3.

**Core changes:** Agreements, one sentence on a grant to oneself (in the next draft); none for the receive-only link (a client and rail Module matter).

## F350. A person's grant may sign that person's receipts and claims (18 October 2026)

**Found by the Damgar round 2 session** (lanes): Money draft 8, rule 7, lets a receipt be signed with a grant key only for a collective (its own grant key, its split service's) and for a payee whose deal names a split service; a person's own grant (a lane) is not listed, so a receipt signed under a person's receipt lane would not count. A claim is "signed by the payer", grant keys unmentioned; Agreements rule 73 makes an act signed under a person's grant that person's own act, so a verifier would have to guess. Both of round 2's lanes rested on rules Money did not state.

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly, 18 October 2026 ("Agreed"):** the project lead's lean: Money rule 7 opens, in the shape the collective case already has: a receipt or a claim may be signed with a grant key its party granted (a person's or a collective's) whose grant reaches that act type; judged as scoped, by Agreements (Identity's scoped key; Agreements rules 73 and 80, F128), a Money-only client showing it unknown, as for a split service today; the limits on a split service's grant key unchanged. *Suggested by the project lead.*

**Core changes:** Money draft 9 (rule 7) and the matching check in the core library.

## F349. The core fixes how its own keys and hashes are written for people: a prefix and a checksum (18 October 2026)

**From** the author's human tests, 18 October (with H38): every identity, act, key and specification shows as 64 hex characters, alike; one pasted where another is meant, or a typo, is not caught. *Asked by Nobody, allegedly:* "All these keys could do with a prefix don't you think?"

**The project lead** first leaned to a cMIP ("textual forms"), the core never reading these strings. **Nobody, allegedly:** "Depends. Why would people want to change prefix?" *The project lead:* almost never: a prefix's only value is that everyone uses the same one; competition there can only break copying between apps; so the lean moved to the core.

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly, 18 October 2026 ("Yes, it is core business, how core keys are labeled."):** the core fixes the textual form of its own objects (an identity, an act, a key, a specification, and any other core hash a person copies): each with its own prefix and a checksum that refuses a mistyped or cut string; and a longer form carrying an identity with where it lives (its homes as hints, the relay transport's address form; hints guide, never decide). Bare hex stays accepted. A kind of object a cMIP defines may register its own prefix in its own text, never reusing the core's. *Suggested by the project lead, the core placement decided by Nobody, allegedly.*

**Settled with the round 2 session, 18 October 2026:** the section sits in the Identity MIR (draft 14, part three, beside Hashes), with one line in MICS v29 and freeze-suite steps; the encoding is bech32m (BIP-350), lowercase, error-detecting, with libraries in every language including C; the labels, decided by Nobody, allegedly ("Yes"; "no Sumerian here or people will get spooked if it ends up in the code"): `mid` an identity (a person's, a collective's, an agent's), `mact` an act, `mkey` a public key, `mspec` a specification, `mwork` a work (the hash of a file's bytes); no form for any secret (signing words stay words); **a label says what was hashed, not what the thing means** (asked by Nobody, allegedly: "should we give collectives their own class, what about other core elements like vows"; decided "Agreed" on the project lead's lean): a collective and an agent are identities (`mid`), a vow, an offer, an agreement and a publication are acts (`mact`); a label per meaning would grow with every type and claim what the encoding cannot check, so meaning comes from the look-up, shown by the app; the where-it-lives form is the short form plus `?r=` hints outside the checksum. *Suggested by the project lead.*

**Core changes:** a short section in a MIR (where it sits, and the encoding's exact choice, for the session writing it to settle with Nobody, allegedly), the core library's encode and decode, conformance vectors for both, every app showing and accepting the forms. Builds on H38.

## F346. Relays and homes store an act signed under a grant, as scoped (17 October 2026)

**Found by the Edubba agents session**, publishing an agent's act under its owner's grant to a real relay for the first time: the relay transport cMIP draft 4 ("Publishing an act", 1) says a relay SHOULD, and a home MUST, check that the act's key is the one the named identity-chain act bound. An act signed with a scoped key has a binding naming a held act of a higher MIR (Identity draft 13, rule 8 and everyday check 2): valid under Identity as far as its signature, status "scoped", judged by Agreements. So any relay holding the grant rejected it ("the binding does not name an identity-chain act of the signer"), and the grantor's homes always did (`relay/src/node.rs`, `check_binding`). Every grant key is affected, a collective's split service included. The core is right; the cMIP predates scoped keys.

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly, 17 October 2026 ("Agreed"):** (a), the project lead's lean: relay transport draft 5 gives the binding check a second case. Where the binding names a grant, a relay that holds the grant checks the signature against the key the grant bound and stores the act as scoped; it never judges whether the act is backed (Agreements', at every verifier). The grantor's homes MUST accept it on the same terms. Not chosen: (b) relays judging the grant in full (relays made Agreements verifiers, refusing what they cannot judge); (c) no binding check for grant-signed acts (junk under invented grants). *Stated cost:* a grantee can fill the grantor's homes, bounded by revocation and each home's policy, as any identity's acts are. *Suggested by the project lead.*

**Core changes:** none; relay transport cMIP draft 5 and the relay's `check_binding`.

## F345. A grant's limits are a cMIP's: a cap per act and an expiry against a named clock (17 October 2026)

**Found by the Edubba agents session:** a grant's limits (fields 3 and 4) are "as the grant's cMIP defines", and none exists; rule 73 judges an expiry "against a time reference", and MOR has no clock of its own. Today the core answers unknown for any grant carrying limits; it can take a limits verdict from a client (`grant_limits`), not yet exposed through WebAssembly.

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly, 17 October 2026 ("Agreed"):** (a), the session's and the project lead's lean: a grant limits cMIP, draft 1, drafted in the agents session as the shared piece Damgar's round 2 lanes reuse (F338's grants: "Per act cap is fine"): a per-act cap in a named units Module, and an expiry judged against a named clock (the timestamp clock Module's stamp of the act, or a block height by the Bitcoin clock on regtest). Edubba feeds the core its verdict; a verifier not knowing the cMIP still answers unknown. Not chosen: (b) limits in the grant's words only, enforced by Edubba's refusal to sign (client conformance; any other verifier would see an act past them as backed). *Suggested by the build and the project lead.*

**Core changes:** none to the texts; the core library's limits input exposed through WebAssembly.

## F344. A correction is labelled by a cMIP; the core already makes the link (17 October 2026)

**Found by the Edubba agents session:** Text draft 9 and Envelopes draft 10 say nothing about corrections; a reference means only "look at this" (Envelopes rule 20), so it cannot say "this corrects that".

**Asked by Nobody, allegedly:** "The core already creates the connection, it simply does not label it, right?" *The project lead:* yes: a new act names the old in `refs`, and the same signer is checkable; what the core does not carry is the link's meaning.

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly, 17 October 2026 ("Agreed"):** (a), the session's and the project lead's lean: a corrections cMIP, draft 1. A correction is a plain text act whose format (Text field 1) is that cMIP's hash, naming the corrected act first in `refs`, signed by the same signer (or by the collective, for a collective's act); clients label it "Correction" and show "corrected by" on the original, which stays signed. A client not knowing the cMIP sees an ordinary post naming the old act. Not chosen: (b) a field in the Text MIR (a core change); (c) a client label from the wording (other clients cannot tell). *Suggested by the build and the project lead.*

**Core changes:** none.

## F343. An act signed under a grant may name its grant (17 October 2026)

**Found by the Edubba agents session** (an agent acting under its owner's grant): Agreements draft 14, rule 73, says a person's grantee signs the person's own act citing the grant on the person's chain (for a collective, rules 58 and 75 ask for the grant in `objects`); Text draft 9, rule 14, lets a text act carry `objects` only where its signer is a collective naming its own latest decision (F334). So a post under a grant is invalid by Text if it cites the grant, and not backed by Agreements if it does not: rule 73's "posting" could never count, for a person's grantee or a collective's. The core does both (`core/src/agreements/view.rs`, backing; `core/src/chain.rs`, `text_pointer`).

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly, 17 October 2026 ("Agreed"):** (a), the session's and the project lead's lean: Text rule 14 opens for one more case: an act signed with a grant key may carry exactly one object, `[grantor, grant]`, the grant its binding names; any other object stays invalid. Not chosen: (b) no posts under a grant (rule 73's posting a dead letter; film 2's agent unshowable); (c) the binding counting as the citation (rules 58, 73 and 75 changed, and a person's grantee act on no chain, so the tie rule could not order it against a revocation). *Suggested by the build and the project lead.*

**Core changes:** Text draft 10 (rule 14's second case) and the matching check in the core library; MICS and the suite to name Text 10 in the freeze-ready drafts.

Two people who fill differently publish two versions of one text, as F322 already shows them. Not chosen: (B) no optional field at all (nothing then discoverable by those fields); (C) a cMIP for marking fields in a text, left to anyone who wants it. *Suggested by the build and the project lead.*
