## F341. An anchoring publication names the key its batch's output is tweaked from (17 October 2026)

**From** `docs/damgar-report.md`, open question 1: the anchoring cMIP draft 5 publishes each batch as `{ 0 offer, 1 batch, 2 leaves, 3 tx, 4 output }`; with pay-to-contract the output is a key tweaked by the batch's root, and without that key (and the script tree's root, if any) no reader can check from the acts alone that the output commits to the root.

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly, 17 October 2026 ("A is agreed"):** (A), the build's lean, already built in the test service: two more fields in the publication, `5` the x-only key the output is tweaked from, `6` the script tree's root where there is one. Not chosen: (B) the key once in the offer's terms (every batch then shares one key on the chain, linking them); (C) out of band (the check would need something outside MOR). *Suggested by the build and the project lead.* A correction of the cMIP's text, not new building for the pool (the pool stays as built, decided the same day).

**Core changes:** none; the anchoring cMIP's next draft (6) carries fields 5 and 6.

## F340. What a person accepts: one sealed list, travelling with the identity (17 October 2026)

*Area: the development layer and presentation (every app). Found by Nobody, allegedly, during Damgar's build: "Found something else that needs to travel, a list of cMIPs and modules the user accepts. Ideally configured from Ummia."*

**The project lead's shape:** one list of the cMIPs and Modules a person accepts, as a private act sealed to their own identity at its homes (as the people cMIP's sealed follow list), read by any app holding the identity on any device; each app applies the part it understands (Damgar the rails and units, Edubba the agreement and split cMIPs, Dub the formats it shows); configured in Ummia, where each specification is seen with its versions, publisher and users before it is ticked; private by default, publishing it the other option (F59). Until Ummia exists, Damgar's Settings write and read the same act.

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly:** "Yes, but I think the other apps should be able to push the decision, in case a user does not use Ummia." So every app may change the list, each for the part it understands, writing a new version on the latest; Ummia shows it whole. *To build with it:* two apps changing it at once must not lose a change (each writes on the latest version it read and checks again before sending; a fork is shown and merged, never silently dropped).

## F339. An app for developers: the Development layer gets its own (17 October 2026)

*Area: presentation and the development layer (a client). Asked by Nobody, allegedly: "Now, do we want an app for developers? So all layers are covered?"*

**The project lead:** Development is the one layer without an app of its own (releases in Edubba; publishing a specification on the command line; the listing as a page of dubsar.org; reports sent through Dub, mirrored from the command line). An app for developers would gather them: publish a specification and see its hash; browse the listing (what exists, by whom, which version); track extensions (which agreements name which cMIPs: "What really matters for MOR2 is the tracking of extensions"); releases, verified file by file; reports sent and answered. Under the working rule (layers apart, talking through MOR), releases would move out of Edubba. A name in the line: Ummia, the master of the tablet house. After Damgar.

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly:** "Yes, I think moving releases and their anchoring out of Edubba makes sense. Log in the devs app, your collectives show there, decide if you act for yourself or a collective." So: releases, and their Bitcoin anchoring (F338), move from Edubba to the developers' app; one logs in with an identity, the collectives it belongs to show, and one chooses to act as oneself or as a collective (a collective's release then proposed there and signed by its members, travelling through MOR). Bitcoin anchoring of a collective's other acts stays in Edubba. *Its name:* **Ummia**, the master of the tablet house. Nobody, allegedly: "Ummia sounds really good yes".

**What Ummia handles** (Nobody, allegedly: "Ummia needs to handle the tracking of modules and cMIPs by pinging at public relays, and the release of cMIPs and modules. As well as giving a path to the MIPs drafting, the bug report and the tracking of extensions and xtypes."): tracking which Modules and cMIPs exist, by asking public relays (the listing, F288, one among any, never a registry); releasing cMIPs and Modules (publishing a specification as its act, its hash shown); a path to drafting MIPs (proposals for MOR 2 are MIPs, drafted layer by layer, a new layer with its own MIP, F286); bug reports (F287, with the GitHub mirror); tracking extensions and xtypes (which agreements name which cMIPs for work the core did not foresee, and which new act types are defined from core pieces, F278: MOR 2's raw material); and releases of code and their anchoring, as oneself or as a collective (above).

**Drafting MIPs** (asked: drafts as documents until ready, or each draft a kind 8 MIP at once, F325): **"A) only once the dev is ready it truly published the specs"**. Ummia helps write a MIP in the MIP shape and publishes drafts as signed long-form documents for discussion; only when its author is ready is it published as a specification (kind 8, naming its creator and the MIP it would replace). *Stated limit, said in Ummia:* tracking extensions sees only public agreements; a sealed agreement's use of a cMIP stays invisible unless someone publishes it.

**Ummia's icon:** a round school tablet of the tablet house, the master's model line above and the pupil's copy beneath (the specification, and an implementation checked against it), in the family's clay on dark. The project lead's lean among that, a reed stylus, a single wedge. Nobody, allegedly: "Agreed".

## F338. Damgar, a money app that gathers money acts for signing (17 October 2026)

*Area: presentation and the development layer (a client). Asked by Nobody, allegedly, after "What was developed for the Money layer?": the Money layer is built and proven in tests (the core's receipts, obligations, payee pointers, vault, releases; the payment cMIP with the Lightning and on-chain rail Modules; split, conversion, units; Bitcoin anchoring end to end on regtest) but no app makes or signs a payment. "I think it deserves demonstrability."*

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly:** "For now, let's give MOR a money app, ideally with a field to pick the cMIPs and modules it accepts. All it does is gather the proposed money acts and offer them for signing." "It does not have to be elegant. It needs to offer ways to showcase how MOR runs." "Yes, we will build a single implementation of it for the tests, but the app is built in a way that can evolve."

**The project lead's notes, agreed in the exchange:** MOR keeps the record and rails move the money, so the app holds no wallet and no Lightning node: money moves in whatever wallet a person uses, and the app gathers, verifies and signs what MOR says about it (requests, receipts, obligations, releases), arriving as acts addressed to the person, as proposals do in Dub's Signing tab. The accepted cMIPs and Modules are a visible choice per person; a money act under one not accepted is shown unknown and not signed, as the core treats a specification it does not implement. Verifying a payment still needs the rail's evidence (a Lightning proof from the payer's wallet; block data for on-chain), so a test network is still needed for real demonstrations, to produce evidence only. Its own app, in the family, for the Mac, Linux and the web, testing only.

**The name.** Proposed: Nigka (níĝ-ka₉, the balanced account), Kubabbar (silver), Damgar (dam-gàr, the merchant); the project lead leaned Nigka. Nobody, allegedly: "Dumgar offers a nicer sound." On the spelling (the word is dam-gàr; "dumu" is "child"): **"Damgar"**.

**The other apps prepare, Damgar signs.** Nobody, allegedly: "It means the other apps need to be able to offer to create the acts without signing them." Dub (a tip, a request to be paid) and Edubba (paying a member's share, a collective's payment) prepare a money act unsigned, as the Claude connector prepares drafts for Edubba; Damgar reads it in plain words, checks it against the rails accepted, and signs or declines. How a draft travels: a shared folder (one device only) or a private act sealed to one's own identity at its homes, as the sealed follow list, which any Damgar picks up with the requests others sent; the project lead leaned to the latter. **Decided:** "Yes, let's have it travel on MOR. It's absurd for the UX, but it's intentional for testing and demonstration." (Clarified: "The absurd was more about Dub not signing a tip": the separation of preparing and signing is the deliberate absurdity, for testing and demonstration.) *Cost:* relays see that a private act exists, never what it holds.

**Anchoring, between the apps.** Nobody, allegedly: "Ok, so anchoring is only for Edubba." The project lead laid out the split under the working rule (layers apart, talking through MOR): Edubba asks for an anchor (the acts most worth anchoring live there: agreements, releases, key changes) and prepares the request; the payment for it arrives in Damgar as a draft sealed to oneself and is signed there; the anchoring service is a third party (for the demo, a test service of ours, labelled). Nobody, allegedly: "Yes, what I meant is that dub only uses the server clock." So Dub uses only the timestamp clock (signed order and the feed's stamps), never Bitcoin anchoring. And in Edubba: "let's have it only as an option for the release and the collectives acts": Bitcoin anchoring offered, as a choice, for releases and for a collective's acts only.

**Splits, and two rounds.** Asked "Do we have a split service we can point to?": the project lead answered no running service, only the split cMIP (draft 3, the rulebook) and a split service simulated in the collective code; a real one, even for testing, is an operator with its own Lightning node and identity, like the anchoring service (both small test services of ours, labelled, talking only through MOR acts). **Decided by Nobody, allegedly:** "Let's do two rounds. The splits are really something that the collectives tab needs to handle. That's where it gets defined. The money app does not even need to approve it, it's an agreement." So: Damgar's first round, plain payments and receipts; a second round with the split service; the split defined in Edubba's Collectives tab as part of the agreement, never approved in Damgar (each owner may see their cut arrive in Damgar's account).

**Round 1's shape** (the project lead's proposal; "Yes it does"): first use, an identity brought by its signing words and the accepted cMIPs and Modules ticked (Lightning rail, on-chain rail, Bitcoin units, split, conversion, anchoring; changeable in Settings); three tabs: To sign (drafts sealed to oneself from Dub or Edubba, and requests from others; each read in plain words with its rail; under a Module not accepted, unknown and not signed), The account (what came in, went out, is owed or pending, each with its proof and whether it verifies), Pay (what to pay, an invoice or an address bound to the payment, paid in one's own wallet, the proof pasted back, checked, the receipt signed); Settings (accepted Modules, the vault limit, homes and relays).

**The network for round 1:** regtest on the author's Mac. Nobody, allegedly: "Regtest is enough, no? It simulates MOR side of things effectively, no?" The project lead agreed: the proofs are real Lightning and Bitcoin proofs on a private chain, so everything MOR does with them (acts, receipts, verification, anchoring) is the same; what regtest does not show is the outside world (other people's nodes, fees, delays, liquidity), and it is demonstrable only from the Mac. Signet, and the phones, for later if wished. Nobody, allegedly: "Round 2 could get something else. As needed… I understand the missing variables and see their value."

**The wallet for round 1:** a plain test-wallet page over the regtest Lightning nodes, separate from Damgar and labelled as a test, used as one would use a real wallet (the invoice copied out of Damgar, paid in the wallet, the proof pasted back). Nobody, allegedly: "Yes, as it would with a real wallet no? No wallet connect, at least not now."

**Its icon:** a Mesopotamian duck weight (a stone weight carved as a sleeping duck, its neck folded onto its body, used by merchants to weigh silver), dark stone on the family's dark, perhaps a thin line of silver beside it. The project lead's lean among a duck weight, a balance, a coil of silver rings. Nobody, allegedly: "I love the duck".

**Round 2: Damgar grants rights to other apps** (asked: "Could Damgar be used to grant right to push to other apps?"). The project lead's reading: Damgar issues a grant (a scoped key, F128, F129) letting another app sign certain money acts itself, within limits (a tip up to an amount, until a date; expiry and limits through the limits cMIP, no sub-granting, F320), revoked by one act in Damgar, the grant travelling on MOR; this removes the detour of a tip through Damgar, by Damgar's leave. To confirm in its session that the grant and limits pieces fit as written. **Decided by Nobody, allegedly:** "Round 2 of course. But it keeps the use of Damgar as safety and separation of layers, but other apps can inherit it from there. Let's build something that can evolve… which in this case is Damgar granting rights for apps that don't exist yet." So the grant names a key, never a known app: any app, including ones not yet written, can hold a scoped key Damgar granted. **Lanes** ("With a cMIP can different lanes be created? With different limits for separate apps?"): each grant a lane with its own scoped key and limits (which money acts, how much per act, to whom, until when), a cMIP giving the vocabulary of limits; per-period totals noted as hard for an outsider to check where acts are private. **Decided:** "Per act cap is fine. It's just another inelegant piece to attempt to show the possibilities and workings of the puzzle." Round 2: lanes with a per-act cap, which act types, and an expiry.

## F337. A collective's rotation: how shares travel, the risks, and saying so (17 October 2026)

*Area: development layer and presentation (Kišib), with a stated cost of the core's split chain key. Asked by the Kišib session; Nobody, allegedly, asked the project lead's view: "how the key shares travel to the one app that executes the rotation".*

**How shares travel (the project lead's view, to weigh against the session's lean):** as they went out at founding. Remote by default: the member who starts the rotation opens Kišib, which makes a one-time key and announces a rotation waiting; each other member types their paper share into their own Kišib, which seals it to that one-time key and sends it. In the same room: the papers typed one after another into one Kišib, nothing sent. A rotation retires the old key, so an old share leaked on the way is worthless once it completes.

**Best practice, to minimise the risks** ("What would be best practice to minimize the risks."): the key alive only in memory, the rotation signed at once, the new shares dealt, the key wiped, never written anywhere; the signed bytes kept for resending, not the key; the cleanest device (the app rather than the web page; ideally a device used for nothing else; the air-gapped way as the gold standard); each member consenting to this rotation, shown in plain words with its fingerprint, the share sealed with that fingerprint; exactly the threshold collected, no more; the waiting one-time key expiring (an hour, say), unused shares unopenable after; rebuild, rotate and deal in one sitting; afterwards new paper replacing old, and the rotation shown in every member's Dub and Edubba.

**The weakness, named** ("Is this a big weakness?"): the rotating device, like the founder's at founding, creates the new chain key and deals its shares, so for that moment it knows the new key whole and, if compromised or dishonest, could keep it and later act alone, past the threshold. A trusted dealer at two moments of a collective's life (founding and each rotation); already the Module's stated price (F97); common to most split-key systems; individual identities unaffected. The project lead's assessment: real but bounded, acceptable for MOR 1 if stated plainly; the cure (generating the key jointly, never whole anywhere) is not practical yet for the hash-based signature the chain key uses: a MOR 2 research item.

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly:** "Kišib needs to lay all this properly to its users." Kišib (and Edubba at founding) says it plainly, before anything is done: who sees the whole key and when, what that device could do if it were not honest, how to choose it, and the practices above.

## F336. Conformance vectors for every layer, and an independent verifier in C, before the freeze (16 October 2026)

*Area: development layer and the freeze. Asked by Nobody, allegedly: "If we were to freeze as is… the first useful test another person could do is to rebuild it from documents, correct?"*

**The finding.** The project lead agreed (the smallest useful rebuild is a verifier: acts in, verdicts out) and checked what a rebuilder could compare against: `core/vectors/` holds part 1 only (tagged hash, CBOR, canonical text, lock, running summary, the three-act sequence, opening acts, the sealed container), checked by the Rust library and an independent Python implementation; missing are signatures (part 2) and verdict vectors for the layers (these exact acts give valid, invalid or unknown: rotations, contests, receipts, succession, Text, Money, Agreements, Development).

**The language.** Asked "So, this other verifier is also in Rust?", the project lead advised another language, for independence (a second Rust verifier would share libraries and habits). Asked "C?", and then "C is very tempting due to popularity with devs.", the project lead agreed: the cryptography and security groups of the first wave read and trust C. *Cost:* a large job; memory safety needs care (strict compiler checks and sanitisers, run on GitHub); the post-quantum pieces come from a pinned library.

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly:** "Yes, let's do it, even if it takes more time, it will show proof of work." Before the freeze: the missing vectors (part 2 and every layer's verdicts), checked by the Python checker as well as the Rust library; then a verifier in C, written only from the frozen-ready texts and the vectors (a session that does not read the Rust code), passing every vector, to go out with the first wave.

**Its scope, and the bar** (asked: "what will the second verifier in C verify? Not the whole code I assume…"). The project lead: the judge only: acts in, with what they rely on (identity chains, receipts, the agreement in force), and a verdict out, valid, invalid or unknown, by the six texts; no networking, no keys or signing, no apps, no cMIPs or Modules; built in layers (the identity chain, acts and signatures; Text; Money; Agreements last), each layer that matches already proof. **Nobody, allegedly:** "Whatever is needed to make it solid all while containing its scope. Someone has to look at it and think 'solid test'." *The project lead's reading of that bar, for its session:* a scope statement first (what it judges, what it does not); every check citing the rule it applies; plain code, no cleverness; strict compiler warnings as errors, sanitizers and fuzzing in its GitHub run; pinned cryptography libraries; one command running every vector into a pass-or-fail table; and a differential run against the Rust library on generated and fuzzed acts, any disagreement a finding.

## F335. MOR Safety is named Kišib: what it must do and what it may do (16 October 2026)

*Area: presentation and the development layer (a client). The rotation app decided in F332 (amended).*

**The name.** The project lead proposed Kišib (Sumerian, the cylinder seal: a person's mark of authority, serious to lose and serious to replace, as the chain key is), Abul (the city gate, the way out) or MOR Keys. **Decided by Nobody, allegedly:** "kišib" (written Kishib where the š will not type).

**What it must do (the project lead's list, the core's emergency acts that today live on the command line):** rotate an identity from its chain key's paper words, keeping every line (Identity rule 35), waiting for the homes' receipts and resending the same bytes, never a second rotation, then showing the new signing words to carry to Dub or Edubba; rotate a collective from enough members' paper shares; escape a contested position (F299); declare a succession, confirmed by the successor (F300); hold nothing between uses (the words typed, used and dropped).

**What it may do (the project lead's list):** check paper words without signing; warn when the identity is rotated by someone else; show the identity's record plainly (homes, receipts, lines, any contest); later, run air-gapped, the signed bytes passed by QR code (the air-gapped signer Module).

**Where it runs.** Options: (a) like Dub, a Mac and Linux app and a web page, testing only, risks stated, usable from any device; (b) the Mac and Linux app only; (c) air-gapped from the start, an offline device signing and an online one carrying the bytes by QR code. The project lead leaned (a) now, (c) later (the one who needs it most may have only a phone; Kišib holds the words only for the minute of the rotation). **Decided:** "A for everyone c as demonstration."

*Taken by the project lead (presentation):* the warning of a rotation made elsewhere belongs to Dub and Edubba, which already look the identity up on opening and before every act ("your key was changed, not from here: open Kišib"); Kišib, holding nothing and running only when opened, cannot watch. *Order:* Kišib is built after real collectives (H18), whose paper shares a collective rotation needs; its prompt "when we get to the tests" (Nobody, allegedly).

## F334. A collective's text names its own latest decision: Text rule 14 opened for rule 58 only (16 October 2026)

*Area: core business (Text and Agreements). Found by the Edubba session building the collectives' Act tab: writing code is also a review.*

**The flaw.** Agreements rule 58 requires every act of a collective to carry `objects` naming `[collective, decision]`, its latest decision on its actions chain, so that nobody can miss it and the tie rule can order it; without it the act counts for nothing. Text rule 14 (F296) makes any text act carrying `objects` invalid. Together, no text a collective posts can ever count. Publications are unaffected (they may carry the pointer). The core's code also let Agreements count such a text although the Text MIR calls it invalid.

**Options (the session's).** (a) Agreements rules 57 and 58 exempt text acts: a collective's texts sit on no chain, counting on their signature; *cost:* not unmissable, and the tie rule cannot order them against a departure. (b) F296's own way: a plain text plus a companion act on the chain naming it; *cost:* two acts per post and a cMIP the collective must adopt. (c) Text rule 14 allows `objects` only where each names the signer's own identity as chain. The session leaned (c).

**The project lead's narrowing.** (c) as worded would let any identity chain its own texts without a cMIP, which F296 deliberately gave up ("a co-written document, a collective's log, a thread in one agreed order" left to a cMIP's companion acts). Narrowed: a text act may carry `objects` **only when its signer is a collective and the one object is rule 58's pointer to its own latest decision on its actions chain**; any other `objects` on a text act stays invalid. F296's reason (no text read as a fork of someone else's agreement) is kept.

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly:** "Agreed" ((c), narrowed). One sentence in the Text MIR, the matching check in the core, and the core's Agreements count brought in line with Text's verdict.

**Built** (pull request 70): the Text MIR draft 9, the core's check, tests. **Found on the way, taken by the project lead as a development-layer correction (no new rule, F295 applied consistently):** Agreements' count still counts acts of other MIRs (Envelopes, Money, Agreements) that their own format makes invalid; many core tests use simplified publications Envelopes' strict format refuses. To be aligned with the freeze-ready code, the tests' publications made real.

## F333. MOR Identities becomes Edubba: agents, identities, collectives (16 October 2026)

*Area: presentation and the development layer (a client). The second app looked at from a user's point of view, after Dub (F332).*

**The name.** Asked for one ("The aim of MOR Identities… was to run tests. Now, it's mostly the Agents' hub, but can also serve as a desk to overview multiple identities, which includes collectives."), the project lead proposed Edubba (Sumerian, "the tablet house", the scribes' school: Dub, Dubsar, Edubba), Ummia or MOR Desk. **Decided by Nobody, allegedly:** "Edubba it is."

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly (the shape):** "one section is agents, one section is identities management for the ones who need it, and one section is collectives. Three tabs." Agents: "First one needs a way to plug the API, Name the Identity and create it. This window loads on first use only, then it become an option if more agents are added. Then the top of the page should always show the list of agents (ID) to select from. Once selected, the bottom half of the page shows the details specific to the agent. Detail, queue of acts to pick from. Option to nudge. And an option to click and be lead to another tab where the user can see MOR as seen by his agents. Then at the bottom a box to prompt the agent directly." Identities: "a repeat of this but without the agents cMIP stuff." Collectives: "shown as id entities, similar to second tab, maybe for each collective a way to see the latest constitutional agreement as a pop up, and if possible show what the user is allowed to act on for each collective, depending on its agreement. For now, the app only acts on text and jpeg envelope but in the future we could extend it so that a user can act for his collective in all their power within the app."

**Added by the project lead, for the brief:** the API key kept in an owner-only file on the Mac, never in the browser, shown by its last four characters only, the daily cap beside it.

**The prompt box: an act or a local chat?** The project lead leaned to an act (a sealed private message from the owner to the agent, signed and kept: an agent acting on MOR has its instructions on MOR; the nudge cMIP answers the owner's own acts at once). *Cost:* every prompt is a signed act. **Decided:** "A)".

**Adding an agent:** an "+ Add an agent" at the end of the list reopens the first-use window (name, identity, terms); each agent signs its own terms. **The API key:** shared by default, each agent with its own daily cap; an agent may be given its own key (the project lead's lean). **Decided:** "Yes".

**The collective client taken in** (the project lead's lean: take it in, at the cost of a larger session). **Decided by Nobody, allegedly:** "Then we need a sub tab for the creation and constitutional actions, signing and so on. What the part of the app I was describing must do is to act for the organization." So the collectives tab has two sub-tabs: acting for the collective (text and pictures for now), and its constitution (founding, members, releases, signing, leaving: the collective client's work).

**The Identities tab's job** (asked by Nobody, allegedly, after using it: "the identity tab does not allow much as of now. should we simply remove it? should we give it features so that it becomes worth keeping?"). The project lead advised keeping it, since Edubba must hold identities that are no agents (an agent's owner signs the prompt box), but not as a second Dub: the identities' back office (releases under one's own name, H16; drafts from Claude; relying on an act, today command line only; the identity's record of routes, homes, keys and lines; later, money), "Dub is for talking, Edubba is for running things". **Decided:** "ok, so it becomes an identity setup page of sorts, and release for the people that could need it."

**"MOR as seen by the agents":** a view, not a fourth tab: from an agent's details, one click opens what it reads (its feed and inbox, those it follows), and back. **Confirmed:** "Correct."

**Asked by the Edubba session, answered by Nobody, allegedly:** whether adding an agent publishes its display name, "(a) Published" (the naming cMIP, at creation; a button for Machine, allegedly); who sets whom an agent follows and whether it reads its feed, "(a) Follow, and read it" (the owner follows for it, privately; a nudge gives Claude its feed, at most ten verified posts not yet answered); on the screenshots, "The identities tab should not have the agents… one for agents, one for secondary identities and one for collectives." Built so (pull request 70, `docs/edubba-report.md`).

## F332. Dub reshaped: an identity window, a public tab, a private tab (16 October 2026)

*Area: presentation and the development layer (clients, a cMIP, a Module). Raised by the human tests (H7, H8, H9).*

**Decided by Nobody, allegedly:** "first is a window where one can create or import an identity. Once that is done we enter the actual app. This first window can be used when wanting to change identity or export it via words." "We need a follow list cMIP I think it's good to have at release." "the app needs to also offer the inbox for private messages." "Let's have separate tabs for public matters and private matters." "Public keeps the feed and the box to post, with option to address the message, no need for the private public switch, the tabs act as one." "Follow, via hashes or via available acts published by others. When following someone, give option to attach a name (for now)." "The private tab show the inbox, and a box to write private messages." On an identity brought in by its words and its encryption key: "Ideally import it. All this business of moving keys won't work like this long term but for now…" On exporting by words: already an option ("Show the carry words").

**Raised by the project lead:** reading sealed messages needs the identity's encryption key, which the carry words do not carry today; a new encryption key published by a second app would fork the identity's versioned acts (carry Module 4.4). The author's answer: the carry brings the encryption key too, for now. *Mechanics to the session (development layer):* a carry Module draft 2 that carries the encryption key with the signing key. *Stated cost:* whoever holds the words can post as the identity and read its private messages.

**What Dub had forgotten (the project lead's list; the author's answers):** replies, "needed as a quick option"; what is addressed to you publicly, no notification hub but a count on each tab, the public tab's "to you" as a filter ("Good, to you as a filter works"); rotation in Dub, "Yes rotation is needed"; muting, "Muting is needed yes"; the feed shows "only simple text acts and supported envelopes (jpg)… The reader is where long form needs to land for now"; money "out of it for now". Your own name: the core already has the act (Identity, name, type 4), whose form and resolution a naming cMIP defines; none is drafted yet. Asked "Can we 'shove' the name, the follow list and the mute in the same cMIP?", the project lead advised two, since a name act names its cMIP by hash and follow and mute will change more often than naming: **a naming cMIP** (a display name: free text, not unique, shown beside the fingerprint, never instead of it) and **a people cMIP** (follow and mute; private by default, a follow list publishable). The author: "Works for me."

**Look and feel:** "It needs a bit of polish too." The project lead proposed the visual bible's world (the background `#06080c`, Inter in light weights, colour only for a layer, red only for a contest, void or stolen key, and its glow rule). The author: "No need for glow. Keep the colors on low saturation." The feel to aim for: "I'm a fan of how Damus feel"; "snort also gave good vibes"; "there is something zen about Damus, amethyst or nostrudel are the opposite end of the feeling" (calm and sparse, not dense and feature-heavy). References: Damus (its public code) and Snort, studied for layout and feel, **no code copied** (Damus is under the GPL). Within Dub's own actions: reply, follow, mute; no likes, no zaps.

**Amended the same afternoon (the session's question on the words):** "The app show the seed for the chain key at creation, once, and that's it. From then on when asked for words, it only gives the signing key. Both should be labeled properly in the app. No rotation from Dub. We can create a MOR Safety app for rotation business." The project lead agreed: Dub then holds no chain key at all (the web client already works so), the chain key lives on paper, and rotation is a separate app's, the natural test-phase client of the air-gapped safety signer. Dub, when its key is no longer current, says so, points to MOR Safety, and takes the new signing words. *Stated cost:* paper words lost means no rotation; as before, but now plainly so. This replaces "rotation in Dub" above and settles the two Dubs' difference.

**Inter bundled** (the Dub session left it for the author's word; the project lead leaned yes, so the look is the same everywhere): "Yes."

**Noticed in passing (cleanup):** the two Dubs differ on the words shown at creation: the Mac shows the signing key's words, the web client the chain key's (with the carry words shown on request). To be made one.

**The follow list:** "Customizable?" Then, on a private default with the choice to publish it (the project lead's lean, under F59): "Agreed". The names attached to followed identities stay in the app either way.

## F331. An owner who is no member signs an ending by chain signature (15 October 2026, afternoon)

*From the build of F330 (`docs/code-after-step-15-part-4.md`, question 1).* F330's consent of owners who are no members, "a signature act the original acknowledges in the ending's history", cannot be met: the ending names its history by its chain act and kept tips, so nothing in that history can acknowledge a signature on the ending itself.

- **(a) taken:** an owner who is no member consents to a fork or closing as a member does, by a chain signature (Identity type 16) with its own chain key naming the ending, judged as every ending signature is (F132, U4), counting at its position in its chain, no later rotation voiding it. One way to sign an ending, whoever signs. **"Agreed."** (Nobody, allegedly.)

- **Q1 (G1), prices:** rule 95 states, where the owners' protection is promised, that the price of what a collective sells, and whether it publishes at all, are the collective's business under its lanes; owners who have no say are protected by sight (rule 57, F318) and the exit, never by a vote on prices; rule 55's commentary no longer equates a release with a zero price (a release ends the claim for good; a price can be raised again). **"Agreed."**
- **Q2 (G2), keepers:** F302 extends to a keeper's record that a rule reads to place an act, or that a version's completeness relies on (rule 47, F314): it keeps the effect the rule gave it whatever the keeper's later rotation disowned, shown as a dispute; a witness chosen in advance cannot un-witness. **"Agreed."**
- **Q3 (G3), a take-up placed late:** a take-up of an issuance that the agreement's time reference places within the period counts, placed by the collective's line or not; a holder's protection is to anchor it, as a buyer anchors a transfer (rule 10); without anchoring, the line's placement, stated. **"Agreed."**
- **Q4 (G4), an owner that is an agreement:** where any holder of a stake is itself an agreement, the issuance offer and the collective's Money acts are public; where a collective seals, "the version in force" is the version the act itself names. **"Agreed."**
- **Q6 (G9, G10), the freeze-ready drafts:** they wait for the code of F292 to F328 and one run of every scenario that code can run, corrected by what the build finds; the Identity, Envelopes and Text scenarios (5.5c, 5.7e, 5.8b, 2.4h, 8.1, the container, the text strictness) run first. **"Agreed."**

G5 (the proxy's "signed nothing on that version" placed at or before the line) and G7's two wordings (rule 96's "same terms" compares the offer's own terms; rule 22 on the law's side only once a profile is published) are mechanics, for the next drafts with the rest of G8 and G10's list.

## F327. Proxies, designed one question at a time (15 October 2026, morning; from F320)

*F320: "Proxies need a core solution." A grant key signs no decision, deliberately, so a proxy is an act of its own.*

**Q1, whose votes:** (a) owners only, votes on the owners' matters under a holders' rule (F307, F309, F310, F311), members voting in person; (b) members too. The project lead's lean: (a), since proxies arise from passive owners, a member's voice is tied to the keys and has the abandonment clause for absence, and members handing their voice to outsiders would open a side door into the constitution. **Nobody, allegedly: "Agreed."** A proxy hands on a holder's vote on the owners' matters only.

**Q2, how long and what it covers:** (a) one vote at a time, naming the version; (b) standing, every owners' vote on the stake until revoked; (c) either, chosen by the owner when signing. With any option: the owner's own vote always beats the proxy's on that matter; revocable at any time; counted where the collective's line placed it (F316's shape); no instructions (an instructed proxy is the owner's own signed vote, which anyone may deliver). **Nobody, allegedly: "C makes sense. Proxies for votes should exist but I don't want them to run wild or do too much. On MOR no real attendance is needed, proxies have little use."** (c), with the rules above; proxies kept narrow.

**Q3, the fence:** a proxy only votes, and only on matters a majority of the stake decides under a holders' rule; never on anything that needs every holder (changing the holders' rule, waiving pre-emption); never on the owner's money or property (taking up an issuance, selling or transferring the stake, signing a deal); no sub-proxies (as no sub-granting); one proxy per owner per stake at a time, a new one replacing the old. "A proxy can add a voice to a majority decision, never give away what is the owner's alone." **Nobody, allegedly: "Good fence."**

*The proxy, as decided (for Agreements draft 14):* an act of a holder naming a proxy identity and a stake, scoped to one owners' matter (a version) or standing over the stake; the proxy's signature act on a version counts as the holder's vote under the holders' rule, where the proxy act was placed by the collective's line before the version's line and not revoked, and the holder signed nothing on that version; revocable by the holder at any time, its revocation placed as the proxy was; inside the fence above. Owners' votes only (Q1). Collectives only, as holders' rules are (F314).

## F326. The machine profile of rule 22 evolves as a specification; the core keeps the promise (15 October 2026, morning; Development Q4)

*From Fable's Development review (D4).* Rule 22 promises that where a cMIP or Module says "anyone can run the same rule", its rule is published as a program for the RV32IM profile, so every client gets the same answer; no profile, program or vector exists, no founding specification makes that claim, and suite 8.2 cannot run. Options: (a) the profile and one compiled rule before the freeze; (b) the promise kept in the core, the founding rules said to be reference implementations, 8.2 deferred. The project lead added: building it now would fix a hurried profile into the frozen core for good; published instead as a specification of its own after the freeze, named by hash, it can be tested, succeeded and adopted by choice, without touching the core. **Nobody, allegedly: "Evolution on this makes sense, freezing it makes little sense."** (b). Rule 22 keeps the promise, the profile named by hash as a specification (not a parameter fixed at the freeze); each founding Module and cMIP with a verification rule says in its text that the rule is a reference implementation, not a published program under rule 22; scenario 8.2 reads "deferred: no published rule in MOR 1"; the first profile is MOR 1's first post-freeze technical specification, open to the first wave.

## F325. A MIP is a specification kind of its own (15 October 2026, morning; Development Q5; with F291)

*From Fable's Development review (D6).* F291 added two kinds (the MICS document, a test suite); a MIP toward MOR 2 (F286, F289) published under kind 4 would read as a MIR to every client and the listing, and lose its creator (kind 4 forbids one). **Nobody, allegedly: "Agreed."** Three kinds, not two: 6 the MICS document, 7 a test suite, 8 a MIP (a proposal toward a successor protocol), which requires a creator and names in a predecessor field the MIR or MIP it forks. The listing shows MIPs in a part of their own, by the MIR they fork and how many fork each.

## F324. The root is the six frozen texts, committed as canonical text (15 October 2026, morning; Development Q3)

*From Fable's Development review (D3) and the step 17 rehearsal (item 5c).* Rule 26 had a client learn the six MIR hashes "from that repository and its own build", which is circular (the author signs both); the repository's files end with a line break canonical text forbids, so a hash of the file is not the specification's. **Nobody, allegedly: "Agreed."** Rule 26: the root is the six frozen texts, published as canonical text; their hashes follow from them; a client ships the texts (or their canonical bytes) and recomputes the hashes it accepts, so two clients can differ only about a text. The frozen specifications are committed as canonical text (no final line break, no trailing space), a repository check refusing any that are not. Suite 8.1: a client recomputes the six hashes from the texts it ships, and shows them.

## F323. A malformed specification is no specification; acts naming it are unknown (15 October 2026, morning; Development Q2)

*From Fable's Development review (D2).* The format called some specifications invalid (field 10 out of order, field 12 wrong) without saying what that does to acts naming them; the core read fields 0 and 12 only, field 10's rule was enforced nowhere. **Nobody, allegedly: "Agreed."** A specification whose content is not in the format is no specification: a verifier holding its content shows it so; an act naming it is unknown to every client, since no client can have adopted it (rule 10); a verifier not holding the content answers unknown, as for any specification it has not adopted. F295's invalid stays for acts outside a specification the verifier implements. The core gains a decoder of the whole format (kinds, references, fee, fields 10 to 13 with their rules), used at adoption and by the listing; `status_cx` unchanged.

## F322. What a specification must carry, per kind (15 October 2026, morning; Development Q1)

*From Fable's Development review (D1).* Every field of a specification but kind, creator and text was optional, so two honest tools publishing one text could make two specifications (different hashes), against F41; rule 26's "a specification that depends on them" could not be checked without field 4; and by F295's letter every founding cMIP's acts were invalid at step 17, their types being defined in a field 3 none carries. **Nobody, allegedly: "Yes."** Part three states per kind which fields are required and which are discovery only: a cMIP filling a task carries field 2; a Module carries field 5; fields 3 (types) and 4 (dependencies) are discovery only, the types a specification defines being those its text defines, as a verifier implementing it knows them (F295's "unknown where the specification is not implemented"); rule 26 reads "names one of them, or a specification whose text depends on them". The publishing rule that makes F41 true: a publishing tool writes every required field and no optional one it cannot fill from the text; two publications of one text that differ in optional fields are two specifications, which a listing shows as versions of one text.

## F321. The MICS opens with the test: law where bytes can prove it, record where they cannot (15 October 2026, morning)

*From the working principle of 14 October and Fable's two additions to it.* **Nobody, allegedly: "I think it's a good statement to lead with."** The MICS's freeze-ready draft opens, before the rules, with two sentences in this sense: MOR is the law where bytes can prove it, and keeps the record where they cannot ("not 'nobody can take unfairly' but 'nobody can take unseen'": the record is what lets the core stay small and right); and a collective's own chain (more generally, the chain that places an act) is where bytes get their order, the one move behind F109, F302 and F316. The exact wording is for the author's reading of the freeze-ready draft.

## Working principle: where MOR is the law, and where it only keeps the record (Nobody, allegedly, 14 October 2026, night)

"MOR isn't the law, it only keeps the record on these matters." And, refining it: "Some promises it can check, but simple ones. 'This is the file that was signed' for example is the law, on MOR." What bytes can prove (a signature, a hash, an order on a chain, a stake paid as written, an offer that reached every holder) the core decides, and every verifier agrees. What bytes cannot prove (a fair price, an intention, whether a promise was kept in the world) the core records, signed and placed, and leaves to the people, their courts and their reputation. The rule behind tonight's answers: check what can be checked, state the rest honestly.

*Fable's two additions, passed on by the author the same night (suggestions, not yet decided):* (1) "'MOR keeps the record' is not a consolation prize; it's what makes the first half safe to be small. A core that tried to judge fairness would have to be big, and would be wrong somewhere; a core that only guarantees this was signed, placed, and seen by everyone it concerned can stay small and be right… That's the legible-greed principle restated as an architecture: not 'nobody can take unfairly' but 'nobody can take unseen'." (F318 is the one place in the round where the record had been kept from the people it concerns, hence fixed rather than stated.) (2) The line has a middle: "facts bytes can prove only once someone has placed them… F109, F302 and the roster answer are all the same move — turning a fact nobody can time into a fact one chain has ordered. 'MOR is the law where bytes can prove it, and the collective's own chain is where bytes get their order' would make the three findings one sentence." Fable's proposal: put the two sentences at the head of the core document (the MICS), so they "give the next reviewer the test before the rules". **For the author to decide** (core business: the MICS).

## F320. Grants against the world's dials (14 October 2026, night; a first poke, before Agreements draft 14)

*The author asked what kinds of granting exist in the world, then: "Do we have all the dials?" The project lead read the grant (type 9) against them: present are scope (fields 1, 6), limits by a cMIP (fields 3, 4), revocation with the tie rule, the grantee's acceptance and own key (F128), traceability, and rule 74 (nobody grants beyond their power). Missing: expiry, proxies, sub-granting, exclusivity, conditions; licences, leases and trusts live in offers, stakes and deals, not grants.* **Nobody, allegedly**, point by point:

- **Expiry:** "Can a grant made to expire with a cMIP?" The project lead: mostly yes, as one more limit of the grant's limits cMIP, judged against a time reference; an act after the date is not backed; the grant goes quiet rather than ending, and stays revocable; a verifier without the cMIP or the time reference answers unknown (F298). To write in draft 14: a sentence saying an act of the grant key outside its cMIP's limits is not backed, as one outside its kinds is not.
- **Proxies:** "Proxies need a core solution." And: "And we agree on proxies. Perfect." Opened by F307 (a passive owner who cannot attend a vote has no way to hand it on; a grant key signs no decision, deliberately). To design, with Fable: the project lead's first shape, a narrow delegation of a holder's vote on the owners' matters, signed by the holder and revocable, separate from grants so grants still decide nothing.
- **Sub-granting:** "I think excluding sub granting is sound." Kept excluded: a grant key signs no grant.
- **Exclusivity:** "Is there a need for exclusivity?" The project lead's lean: no in the core; unenforceable for a person (no verifier proves the absence of another grant), a deal term where wanted. *Explored at the author's request* ("I'm just curious why would someone want it… on Mor"): an agent who invests in an artist, or a distributor paying an advance; both promises (no other agent appointed; an exclusive sales window), which are deal terms, not grants, and a voluntary narrowing of MOR's openness. **Nobody, allegedly: "MOR isn't the law, it only keeps the record on these matters."** Not in the core: exclusivity is a promise written as a deal term, its breach visible on the record.
- **Conditions:** "Agreed on conditions": a cMIP's (an escrow's "only once X").
- **Licences, leases, trusts:** "Agreed": not grants; offers, stakes and deals.

The cost of (a), stated: Agreements draft 14, the code for F302 to F318, tests and freeze scenarios, and Fable's attack on it again before the freeze-ready drafts. **Nobody, allegedly: "A) and we haven't even talked grants yet,.. something I want to go poke when we can."** Map Q7 (a declared tier) and Q8 (a fork share rule): not taken, as the map and Fable lean, the owners' matters and the fork act's field 5 reaching what they were for.
