## F318. Owners without a say see the collective's money acts (14 October 2026, night; Agreements round 2, Q5)

*From Fable's second Agreements review (A2-5), on the project lead's objection 5.* Spending the reserve (F312) is the members' business, and members may pay themselves from it; a prohibition ("the reserve pays no member without the owners' agreement") is not decidable, money at the collective's pointer being fungible and a member's service indistinguishable from a supplier. What was missing is sight: under rule 57 a collective's act is done once sealed to every member, or public, so holders who are no members saw none of its Money acts. **Nobody, allegedly: "Agreed."** For a collective whose stake in itself has holders who are no members, an act in its Money lane (a receipt, an obligation, a payout, a payee pointer) is done only once sealed to every holder of that stake, members and others, or public; the collective's client makes them public by default. Cost: more recipients per sealed act, or publicity; such a collective loses privacy from its owners only. "The right of exit means little to an owner who cannot see why to leave." Rule 57 for the next Agreements draft; a freeze scenario: a payout sealed to members alone binds no one.

## F317. The owners' choice of operators binds through the keys (14 October 2026, night; Agreements round 2, Q4; F311's stated cost)

*From Fable's second Agreements review (A2-4), on the project lead's objection 4, correcting the project lead's reasoning in F311.* Rule 93 punishes a rotation made without the signatures the constitution requires; it cannot compel a rotation refused. A clone removing the operator-members needs a rotation (rule 69), which needs the chain key the operators hold; refused, the clone is complete and in force nowhere, the collective is not broken and runs on under the old members; a fork and a closing need their chain signatures; the recovery path is for a lost share, not a refusal. **Nobody, allegedly: "Agreed."** F311's stated cost: the owners' power over membership binds only through the keys; where the key holders refuse the rotation, the vote stands complete and unexecuted, visible to every reader of the chain; the owners keep the owners' matters (they can refuse every fee, issuance and sale) and the exit, not replacement. A constitution that counts membership among a stake's holders SHOULD also put a share of the chain key, or the recovery path's custodian (rule 59, `recovery = [0, custodian, grant]`), under the owners' control, so the holders' rule can produce the rotation; the founding client warns when it does not (client conformance).

## F316. A transfer counts for the owners' matters where the collective's line placed it (14 October 2026, night; Agreements round 2, Q3)

*From Fable's second Agreements review (A2-3), on the project lead's objection 3.* A transfer takes effect on the seller's own line (rule 10, F217) and completes with the buyer's signature act, neither on the collective's chain; counting "every transfer held" gives two verifiers two rosters, and a seller who voted and a buyer who bought after could both count. The code reads holders from field 7 alone (`pay_signers`), so F282's buyer is never asked: text without code. **Nobody, allegedly: "Agreed."** F302's shape for transfers: a transfer counts for a collective's owners' matters only where the collective's line placed it (an act of the collective acknowledges it, or a record names it among transfers registered); until then the seller holds, for the collective, what field 7 and the transfers already placed say; a vote counts the roster at the line that puts the version in force; a transfer placed but not held is unknown (F298, F304). Money follows transfers as rule 10 says, unchanged. In a deal, the version carrying an owners' matter names the transfers it counts. The buyer accepts the holders' rule of the version in force at the placement. **Stated cost:** a buyer has no vote until the collective places the transfer; members can delay a hostile buyer's vote, visibly, bounded by the next record any member draws. `pay_signers` is built against this rule.

## F315. Pre-emption: the core checks that the offer reached every holder; price and absence are stated costs (14 October 2026, night; Agreements round 2, Q2)

*From Fable's second Agreements review (A2-2), on the project lead's objection 2.* Pre-emption (F309) keeps every holder's proportion available on the same terms; it cannot protect value (a token price dilutes an owner who cannot or does not pay; the core cannot judge a price) nor an owner who misses the period. The candidate repairs were refused: "below the last price needs every holder" is not decidable by a verifier and restores the veto; "a right that stays open" restores the veto or leaves a floating claim nothing can value or close. **Nobody, allegedly: "Agreed."** The core checks that an issuance's offer reached each holder (public, or sealed to that holder, as a declaration must reach the member it names, F189) and names its period on the agreement's time reference; an offer that did not reach a holder starts no period for that holder; the period's lapse is the time reference's answer, a fact the caller states, as for every time reference today. **Stated cost, as in law:** an owner who does not take up its proportion within the period, or has no money for it, is diluted. An owner who vanished for good is reached by F313. A cMIP may offer more as a term of the issuance.

## F314. A deal's version, once relied on, stays complete; holders' rules are a collective's only, for now (14 October 2026, night; Agreements round 2, Q1)

*From Fable's second Agreements review (A2-1).* A deal has no line (Q28); a version is complete with every party's signature (rule 47); under F297 a party who rotates and disowns their signature uncompletes it for everyone, the parent returning to force with purchases and splits already made under the version. A hole older than F307; F302 does not reach it. **Nobody, allegedly: "Agreed."** (a) A deal's version, once any party's or keeper's later act relies on it (a purchase naming it, a split under it, a keeper's record of it, a later version cloning it), keeps its completeness for everyone, the disowned signature shown as a dispute: "what others relied on stands", the relying act as the place; checkable, since the relying act names the version. (b) Until keeper records (type 2, format open) give a deal a line, the holders' rule (F307) is a collective's only. For a collective, an owners' vote registered by its line is kept by F302, with C5's stated cost (a thief's vote with a stolen signing key stands once registered; it can pass only what hits every holder alike); owner votes are not signed by chain key.

## F313. A declaration of absence may name a holder who is no party; F279's way out made true (14 October 2026, night; Agreements round 2, Q6)

*From Fable's second Agreements review (`docs/reviews/fable-step-15-agreements-round-2.md`, A2-6), its lead finding.* F279 promised that a departed holder who vanishes "does not block the collective's fees, because its stake moves: declared absent, to its stake successor, or … by outcome 1 or 2". But a declaration of absence names "a party to the agreement" (rule 87; the declaration's field 2), and a departed holder, a stake successor and a buyer are no parties (field 22; rules 83, 10); the library refuses (`declaration_as`). So one holder without a say who vanished blocked every fee change for good (F270, F282), and with F310 every sale, release and fork; the members' collective failed the author's condition (F306) on that one case already, before any of the shapes map's additions. **Nobody, allegedly: "Yes."** Rule 85 and the declaration's format: a declaration may also name a holder of the collective's stake in itself who is no party, under the clause in force; against such a holder its outcomes are the stake-moving ones only (succession as rule 89 says; outcome 1 or 2 where the holder's plan names no stake successor), never 0, 3 or 4; the authority and the absence-proof cMIP are the clause's as for a party. Rule 61's coverage unchanged. A freeze scenario: a departed holder vanishes; declared absent, the stake passes to the plan's successor, who signs the sale; with no plan, outcome 1, and the sale proceeds.

## Agreements round 2, Q7, taken by the project lead under the working practice (development layer)

A2-9: the air-gapped Module grants F299's exception (a position contested at a self-hosted home) only on presentation of the competing rotation, checked offline under the chain key it revealed (its signature verifies, its previous act is the same, it is not the rotation the device signed); the online side's word alone does not open it. A refusal (rule 29's first case) stays unprovable from bytes, told to the human as today. Identity rule 29's wording follows.

## F312. A collective may keep money for itself, off the top of its own plan (14 October 2026, late evening; shapes map Q6)

*From the shapes map (Q6).* With income flowing through a stake to its owners, a collective had no named way to keep reserves or reinvest. Options: (a) the split plan may name the collective itself off the top, that part kept as its own money and never split again; (b) another identity off the top, such as an operating account run under a grant; (c) no stake in itself, owners paid by decision. Under F280 the collective's part is off the top: raising it pays the stakes less, so it is an owners' matter (holders' rule, F307, or each signing); spending it is the collective's business. **Nobody, allegedly: "Yes, stakeholders would decide so when needed. A."** One sentence in rule 22 for the next Agreements draft, after checking how a split service treats a payout to the collective's own pointer, which the text does not yet say.

## F311. Owners may choose who runs the collective, as a choice at founding (14 October 2026, late evening; shapes map Q5)

*From the shapes map (Q5).* In MOR only members chose members. Options: (a) as an option, the power over membership (a constitutional change rule) may be counted as a stake's holders' rule by share; (b) no, owners consent on what they own and leave if they disagree (a company shaped as a limited partnership). The map leaned (b), with least confidence, on the reason that a change of members needs a rotation of keys the operators hold, so an owners' vote the keys can ignore is a promise the core cannot keep. **The project lead disagreed with that reason:** the core already enforces a constitution against its key holders by the broken collective (rule 93): a rotation moving keys without the signatures the constitution requires breaks the collective, its acts count for nothing until rolled back, visibly; so an owners' vote over membership is kept exactly as the members' own rules are kept, not by force but by making defiance void and visible. Cost: a majority of capital could remove those doing the work, chosen at founding. **Nobody, allegedly: "As long as it is a choice. It really all depends on balance… some people need to protect their work, some people need to protect their investment because it outweighs the work done until that point."** (a), as an option: a collective may, in its constitution, count the power over membership among a stake's holders by share; the default stays members choosing members. For the next Agreements draft: rules 61 and 65 read again (coverage and counting where the constitutional rule is a holders' rule), and rule 93's broken collective named as how it is kept.

## F310. The owners' matters: a sale or release of works, and a fork's founding plans (14 October 2026, late evening; shapes map Q4; answers Agreements Q7 and Q8)

*From the shapes map (Q4) and Fable's Agreements review (A10, A11).* Two side doors paid owners without a say less with no change to the plan: a collective selling or releasing the works its income comes from ("25% of nothing", A11), and a fork whose successors' founding plans take more off the top (A10). Options: (a) both owners' matters; (b) only the fork, a sale a stated cost; (c) neither. **Nobody, allegedly: "A."** A transfer or release of a work by a collective, and a fork's completeness where a successor's founding terms pay a kept holder less than the original's agreement in force at the line (rule 48's "paid less", F274, F280), need the agreement of the stake's owners who have no say: by their holders' rule (F307), or, where there is none, each signing; F279's way out against a holder who vanished. The parked Agreements Q7 and Q8 are answered. For the next Agreements draft: the A11 check on transfers and releases, the A10 check on forks (`stakes_paid_less` restricted to kept holders), and their freeze scenarios.

## F309. Issuance: new owners, every holder diluted alike, pre-emptive by default (14 October 2026, late evening; shapes map Q3)

*From the shapes map (Q3), answering the author's "So what we are missing is the issuance of new shares…".* A new owner could join a stake only with every existing owner's signature (rule 8; F274's "another share added"). Options: (a) issuance as its own change, every existing share shrinking in the same proportion, decided by the stake's holders' rule (F307; every holder where there is none), each existing owner first offered their own proportion on the same terms, the pre-emption waivable only by every holder; (b) the same with pre-emption left to cMIPs, protecting no one the core counts against a majority issuing to itself; (c) no. **Nobody, allegedly: "Agreed, any other form needs a cMIP."** The core's issuance is (a); any other form of issuance (staged, vesting, other offer terms) is built by a cMIP within it, never around the pre-emption. The offer period needs a time reference (a cMIP), needed only by a collective that issues by vote. For the next Agreements draft: issuance and its offer act; rules 8 and 45 and F274's list read again (a share added by issuance under the holders' rule is not "paid less" in F274's sense; one added any other way still is).

## F308. A rule may count shares of a stake, and may combine rules (14 October 2026, late evening; shapes map Q2)

*From the shapes map (Q2; Fable's hook 1).* Every vote among members was one voice per member (rule 65). Options: (a) two new optional forms of rule: "holding together at least this share of this stake" (weights read from the stakes, never written as free numbers) and "all of these rules" (for instance a majority of members and owners holding 60%), one voice per member staying the default; (b) no. **Nobody, allegedly: "It's optional, agreed."** For the next Agreements draft: the two forms; rule 65's counting for voices that are gone (flaw C) restated in shares; rule 61's coverage reaching a member whose share alone can block a rule; the succession plan's seat "voting weight" given this meaning or dropped. The release rule's threshold (field 24, counting heads, open to a holder splitting its share among identities it controls; the map's side finding) is counted by share with this form. *Confirmed by Nobody, allegedly: "By head was a real mistake all along…"*

## F307. A holders' rule per stake: owners without a say may agree together, if they accepted it (14 October 2026, late evening; shapes map Q1)

*From the shapes map (`docs/collectives/shapes-map-draft-1.md`, `docs/collectives/shapes-map-report-for-the-lead.md`, Q1), F306.* Owners without a say (a member who left, a successor, a buyer of part of a stake) each signed personally every change paying them less (F270, F282), so a company or a venture with many passive owners could not change a fee, keep money for the business or issue a share. Options: (a) a holders' rule per stake, in two forms, "every holder" (the default, today's rule) or "owners holding at least this share", changed only by every holder, the release rule's shape (field 24) widened; (b) no, companies wait for MOR 2; (c) only for buyers; (d) buyers made members (each trade a rotation of the keys). The project lead's lean, with the map's: (a). **The cost, stated to the author in an example:** Dana holds 10% of a stake under a 60% rule; owners holding 70% take 5% off the top for a studio; she voted no; today her no blocks it, under (a) it passes and she is paid a little less without signing. She keeps: no singling out (the change hits every owner of the stake alike), a vote in proportion to her share, her proportion (issuance with pre-emption), the exit. She loses the personal veto, and bears her share of the majority's judgment. Not fixed by any rule: the majority paying a service it owns outside MOR, a stated cost judged by reputation. **The author: "If the veto mattered to her she would not have agreed to the 60% rule."** The rule binds only those who chose it: a member who signed the agreement carrying it, a buyer who bought under it; adding one later needs every holder. **Nobody, allegedly: "Agree."** For the next Agreements draft: the holders' rule per stake, reaching only changes that hit every owner of the stake alike, binding only holders who accepted it by signing or buying; F279's sentence becomes "No advance permission reaches what one holder is paid unlike the others"; who holds what at the moment of a vote settled so that a seller and a buyer never both count.

## F306. Collectives are thought through before the freeze: the shapes mapped, the common language found (14 October 2026, evening)

*Following the open thread below.* The author: "I think it shows that collective need a serious dose of thinking." Options laid out by the project lead: think it through before the freeze, or freeze MOR 1 with collectives as cooperatives and the cost stated, companies waiting for MOR 2. **Nobody, allegedly: "Think it through before the freeze. Map the shapes people actually use (a band, a cooperative, a company, an investor-backed venture) against the core, and find the smallest thing the core must allow so each can be built as a cMIP above it. The Company cMIP sketch is the first of those maps. This is the right approach. The core does not need to implement them all, it needs to find the common language that the various cMIPs need to speak."** A new piece of work before the freeze-ready drafts: the shapes of collectives mapped as cMIP sketches against Agreements draft 13, and the core's common language drawn from what they share. Agreements Q7 and Q8 (A10, A11) are answered by it, not before; F270, F274, F279, F280 and F282 are read again in its light, not undone in advance.

*A condition, added the same evening by Nobody, allegedly: "One condition on collectives: some form of collective is possible at the core without cMIPs."* The core alone, with no cMIP, keeps at least one working form of collective; the shapes built as cMIPs sit above it, never in place of it.

*Fable's notes for the map, passed on by the author the same evening:* the places where the core says "one way": rule 65's counting (one voice per party; no weighting unless a term may say so), rule 64's computed tier (a class vote over a constitutional matter fits nowhere), rule 91's fork shares (by stake in the collective itself, a cooperative's default), rule 85's abandonment (one clause, a cooperative's; a company's "director removed by board" is a judicial change needing every member). Fable's lean: if the core keeps one form, keep the cooperative, "the one where every protection is already proven", with three hooks: (1) weighted or classed voices in rule 65 as a term, not a cMIP's fact; (2) a declared tier where the computed one would be wrong, carried in the mark so a false claim still sinks the clone (F104); (3) a fork share rule in field 5 that may follow something other than the stake in itself. "With those three, I think a company runs above it; without them, a company cMIP has to work around the core, which is what the admission test forbids." *The project lead's addition, to test:* the three hooks concern members; holders without a voice still hold a personal signature over every change paying them less (F270, F282), so a fourth hook may be needed: a collective way for such holders to consent (a stake-weighted vote accepted by signing or buying), or a voice for share buyers through hook 1; either reopens F279's "No advance permission reaches what a stake is paid".

## Open, not decided: owners without a voice, and a Company cMIP (14 October 2026, evening; Agreements Q7 and Q8 parked)

*From Fable's Agreements review, A10 (a fork's successor plan dilutes a departed holder unchecked) and A11 (a sale or release of works pays departed holders less, unseen). The author: "Dilutions is a tough subject. Let's slow down…" Then the thread, in his words and the project lead's laying out.*

- The author: "I'm wondering if this was a mistake: [a change paying a departed holder less needs their signature]. A departed holder owns a percentage of the stake at the moment they left and gave up right to speak in the collective. Why would the stake remain the same forever…"
- Laid out: three shapes. (a) A share of the collective, frozen, with a personal veto (today, F270 to F282). (b) Royalty-like stakes in the works existing at departure, the future free. (c) Company shares: diluted equally with the members, a signature only against unequal treatment (rule 68's "no term treats it worse than a member's"), self-dealing (costs paid to a members' own service) a stated cost, since the core has no court.
- The author: "So what we are missing is the issuance of new shares…" Confirmed in the text: "another share added or raised" is on F274's paid-less list, so no new member's stake without every non-member holder's signature. Issuance would be its own act, everyone diluted in proportion; the classic abuse (the majority issuing to itself) answered in companies by pre-emptive rights.
- The author: "Companies have two aspects, the owner and the operational powers. Collectives bundle them together, right?" Laid out: MOR's formats keep them apart (stakes; areas, grants), members bundle them; a company's non-operating owners still vote on owner-level matters (issuance, sale of assets, mergers, dissolution); a departed holder has no vote, and the F270 signature is a crude stand-in for it, a personal veto rather than a proportional vote.
- The author: "So to make a company we need these two, separated. The question is, do we want to make companies?" The project lead: MOR should let people make companies, cooperatives and bands, not choose among them (his working rule: has the core defined how the choice is made by participants?). A suggestion followed (a departed holder bound by the agreement last signed), **then withdrawn by the project lead as it contradicts F279: "No advance permission reaches what a stake is paid."**
- The author: "Let's try this to make sense of it… a Company cMIP. Which adds to what the core offer. Then we understand what needs what…" The sketch: shares, board, transfers are core and fine; dividends and costs (F270), issuance (F274) and a shareholders' vote are blocked by the personal signature of every holder without a voice. **The exercise's finding:** under F282 every buyer of part of a stake holds a personal veto over every change to the plan, so a company with many passive owners cannot exist on MOR today. What a Company cMIP would need from the core: a way for an agreement to set a collective way of consenting for holders without a voice (a stake-weighted vote, say), accepted by signing (a departed member) or by buying (a share buyer); which reopens F279's sentence, F270, F274, F280 and F282.
- **Parked at the author's word: "Let's pow wow again a bit later today."** Agreements Q7 (A10) and Q8 (A11) wait on it.

## F305. A confirmed identity-level succession nominates a successor to the seat; the stake stays (14 October 2026, step 15, Agreements Q4)

*From Fable's Agreements review (A5); F300 applied to rule 84.* Rule 84's fallback ("the identity-level succession declared in a rotation applies") was undefined and uncoded. **Nobody, allegedly: "Yes."** Rule 84: where no plan exists, a succession the party declared under Identity and its successor confirmed (F300) makes the successor a nomination for the party's seat, approved as rule 64 says (rule 83: no automatic seat without consent given in advance by everyone still counted); the stake stays with the party's identity, which Identity keeps alive, paid at its pointer, until a transfer or the abandonment clause moves it; a succession not confirmed nominates nobody. A freeze scenario: a party with no plan and a confirmed successor gets a nomination, never an automatic seat; an unconfirmed one nominates nobody.

## F304. "Made after" is unknown while the line's history is not held (14 October 2026, step 15, Agreements Q3)

*From Fable's Agreements review (A4); F298 applied to collectives.* The library read an act missing from a line's rebuilt history as "after" even when the history had holes, and "after" fed the tie rule (void) and voices (gone): a verifier with one hole voided acts another verifier kept. **Nobody, allegedly: "Yes."** "Made before, made after", 1: while an act the line's history names is not held, whether an act is before that line is unknown, and nothing is void on it. Rule 58: an action citing a decision is unknown while the decision is not held, and counts for nothing where it is held and is no decision of the collective. A fork's or closing's "not complete" becomes unknown where the cause is an unheld act; "fails closed" names unknown. The code threads the history's `missing` through `before` (one signature and its callers).

## F303. An ending's finality rests on chain keys and the collective's own chain (14 October 2026, step 15, Agreements Q2)

*From Fable's Agreements review (A2).* A complete ending is final (F131, IT1), but each successor accepted its debts by an ordinary signature act, and a closing's "owes nothing" rested on creditors' receipts and releases, all acts of other chains judged by their status now, so a successor or a creditor could unmake a final ending by a later rotation. The author asked whether F297 was a wrong step; the project lead's answer: no. The library already judged those acts by their current status before F297, so a disowned release could reopen a closed debt already; F297 removed only the accidental shield of an acknowledgement, which was the hole in the exit. F297 (another's evidence counts only while its signer stands by it) and F302 (what a collective wrote onto its own chain stays placed for it) are a pair, and this applies them to endings. **Nobody, allegedly: "Is F297 the problem? Did we take a wrong step there? If not, accepted."** (a) A successor signs a fork as a member does, by a chain signature with its own chain key (Identity type 16), reversing F132's "reading, to confirm"; cost: one chain-key ceremony per successor per fork. (b) A closing relies on a receipt or a release of another identity only where an act of the collective in the closing's history acknowledges it (or the receipt is the collective's own); it is then placed by the collective's line, and F302 keeps it; one nobody acknowledged is shown and the closing waits. The same reading, one sentence each, for a stake transfer's buyer signature and a grant's acceptance.

## F302. What a collective's line placed stays placed for that collective (14 October 2026, step 15, Agreements Q1)

*From Fable's Agreements review (`docs/reviews/fable-step-15-agreements.md`, A1).* Under F297, a member's resignation (or a declaration of absence, the signatures completing it, or the signatures its field 1 is checked against) disowned by its signer's later rotation would decide nothing; the record that registered it would then be no line, the clone it wrote not in force, the departure never taken, and every later record would find the wrong parent: one member's theft protection unwinding a collective's year. The library applied Identity's current verdict to these at every evaluation; only signatures had C5. **The rule, F297's exception for Agreements:** an act of another identity that a line of a collective (a record, a rotation, a rollback) names, registers, or relies on to count what it registers keeps, for that collective, the effect the line gave it, whatever its signer's later rotation kept or disowned; its status under Identity is unchanged, and every client shows it as a dispute on the line. C5 extended from signatures to resignations, steppings down, declarations and their completing signatures, and the signatures field 1 is checked against. The line is the collective's own act (F109; rule 34; "what others relied on stands"; rule 87's "a later act of the party never undoes what was put in force"). **Stated cost:** a thief holding a member's signing key can resign the member; once the collective's line registers it, the member's rotation does not undo the departure for the collective; the member returns by a constitutional clone naming them. **Nobody, allegedly: "Cost is acceptable. Accepted."** The code follows (`record_eval_inner`, `declaration_as`, `recovery_signatures`, `rollback_departures` take `sig_counts`'s test for registered acts and show the dispute), with the freeze scenario A1 describes.

Q5 (A6): F295 for Agreements acts at the core verifier (a dispatcher over every Agreements type's decoder, `Unsupported` for formats still open, invalid for a retired or undefined type) is its own pull request, made where the suite runs, after Development's segment, since the open formats are Development's to fix first. Q6 (A7, A8): type 11 is retired, as the type table says (rule 77 and rule 76 follow, the constant goes); Agreements cites Money draft 7's rule numbers; the verdict words "a verifier refuses it", "fails closed", "refused rather than guessed" become *unknown*; the suite's three concurrency-rule sentences are corrected to rule 90's default. All for the next consolidated revision.

## F301. A rail Module's kind and what its proof binds are fields of its specification (14 October 2026, step 15, Money Q1)

*From Fable's Money review (`docs/reviews/fable-step-15-money.md`, M2).* Whether a rail is a request rail or a push rail decides, for a push-rail payment two holders received, purchase or refund (rule 18, F128 W4); whether its proof binds payee, purpose and the previous hop decides which act settles a disagreement (rule 11, F151). Both lived in each rail Module's code (`RailModule::kind()`, constants in `modules/lightning` and `modules/onchain`), the text marking them "FORMAT OPEN" (F140 item 1): a rule resting on a fact nobody can check from the acts and the specifications they name. **Nobody, allegedly: "Agreed."** Development's specification format gains, for the Module kind, two fields: the rail's kind (request or push) and what its proof binds (a set: payee, purpose, previous hop). The payment cMIP reads them from the specification, never from code; the Lightning and on-chain rail Modules are republished with them. A format change before the freeze, written with F291 in Development's freeze-ready draft. Money's mechanics M3 ("pending", not "unrecorded"), M4 (a payer's client that cannot anchor on the payee's clock says so before paying) and M5 (an obligation's states shown by clients that read Agreements; a Money-only client shows them unknown; a Money view for MOR 2) are for the freeze-ready draft.

## F300. A succession is declared by the rotation and confirmed by the successor (14 October 2026, step 15, Identity Q4)

*From Fable's Identity review (I4).* A succession needed one side only: a stranger's rotation could name a well-known identity as successor and clients would show it; and where a collective's party has no succession plan, the identity-level succession applies (Agreements draft 13, rule 84), so a seat with its stakes and duties could pass to an identity that never agreed. Beside it: rule 14 (a link needs both sides, "otherwise anyone could claim to be anyone"); F279 (abandonment moves a stake only by succession); F282 ("A new agreement is born. All signatures are needed"). Stated honestly: confirmation does not stop a thief holding the chain key naming and confirming an identity of their own, the stated cost of a lost chain key. The author asked when the successor confirms: after the rotation, since the confirmation names it; at any time before the succession is used, usually at once, arranged together, or later, even after the owner is gone, since it needs nothing from the owner. **Nobody, allegedly: "Ok, confirmed."** A succession to a MOR identity is declared by the rotation and confirmed by the successor (a link confirmation or a witness act naming the rotation); clients show it as declared until confirmed and follow it only confirmed; Agreements' rule 84 fallback passes a seat only to a confirmed successor. A succession to another protocol is confirmed as that protocol's link cMIP says. Scenario 8 gains step 8b. *Presentation, for clients:* telling an owner whose named successor has not yet confirmed.

## F299. A contested self-hosted position is left by the escape, and the device signs it (14 October 2026, step 15, Identity Q3)

*From Fable's Identity review (I3).* At a self-hosted home no third party fixes an order, so a thief with the chain key and the owner each serving a rotation at one position leave it contested for good (`tally`, `Operator::Own`), not "the thief wins at once" as rule 4 says. The owner's way out, the escape with both keys (section 11), exists; but rule 29 allows the second signature only after a rotation "refused by the homes that count", a self-host refuses nothing, and the air-gapped Module, which enforces rule 29, would not sign it. **Nobody, allegedly: "Agreed."** Rule 4: two rotations served at one position of a self-hosted identity, by a thief and the owner, are a conflict nobody can settle; the position is contested for good, and the owner leaves it by the escape. Rule 29's second exception: "refused by the homes that count, or contested at a self-hosted home". The air-gapped Module signs in that case; the gauntlet's self-hosted run gains a check (scenario 5, step 7f).

## F298. A verifier that does not hold what a rule needs answers unknown (14 October 2026, step 15, Identity Q2)

*From Fable's Identity review (I2), E11 of the Envelopes review, and the step 17 rehearsal's dark site.* The library answered void when it could not rebuild the line to a kept tip (one act of the line not held), invalid for a scoped key whose grant it had not held, and invalid for acts bound to a verifiable rotation of a chain it could not resolve (a genesis under a scheme the client lacks); each flipped as acts arrived. After the author's first rotation in the rehearsal, the reader showed the first post void and dubsar.org went dark. Options: (a) unknown wherever a verifier lacks what a rule needs, with void only for an act shown outside every kept line rebuilt and invalid only for a rule the verifier can apply and sees fail; (b) as built. Beside it: F159 (an unfetchable link is unknown, never invalid), T3 (invalid is for a rule the verifier can apply), the plan's order-independence rule, F292. **Nobody, allegedly: "Agreed."** Identity states once, after rule 35: a verifier that holds the acts a rule needs answers as the rule says; one that does not (a kept tip whose line it cannot rebuild, a scoped key whose installing act it has not held, a chain it cannot resolve) answers unknown, never valid, void or invalid; void needs every kept line of the rotation rebuilt and the act on none of them. The "same answer" paragraph lists the signer's sequence among what two verifiers must hold. The code follows (`judge`'s fourth answer, the scoped branch, `status_cx` on `Stop::Unknown`), with three tests. Separately, an instrument: the reader and the display client fetch the signer's sequence as the feed and the release verifier do (I8, the rehearsal's fix), so the site does not go dark at all; the shorter kept-ancestry proof (peaks and a path) can follow after MOR 1 without changing any act.

## F297. Evidence counts only while its signer's chain keeps it (14 October 2026, step 15, Identity Q1)

*From Fable's Identity review (`docs/reviews/fable-step-15-identity.md`, I1), its lead finding: a hole in the way out.* Rule 34 keeps an act voided by its signer's rotation "valid and shown as disputed" when another identity acknowledged it, which protects those who relied on the signer's own acts. But objections, absence statements, auditors' cosignatures and acknowledgements fell under the same words, and the library read disputed as standing (objections, absence statements) or never checked the signer's rotation at all (cosignatures, acknowledgements). So a thief with a home operator's signing key signs an objection to the owner's homeless rotation, an accomplice witnesses it, the operator rotates and disowns it, and the exit is blocked for good: two signatures, no chain key, against the very owner the homeless path exists for. The same keeps a forged log summary "audited" and a thief's post disputed for ever. Receipts were closed for this in round 2 (F95); the other four never were. **The rule:** an act of another identity that a MIR uses as evidence (a receipt, an objection, an absence statement, a cosignature, an acknowledgement) counts only while its signer's chain keeps it valid. One voided by its signer's rotation is shown, as a dispute, and decides nothing, acknowledged or not. A signer's own acts keep rule 34 unchanged: disowned but relied on, they stay visible as disputed. Honest evidence loses nothing: a rotation keeps everything its signer does not disown. **Nobody, allegedly: "Yes, approved."** Written once, generalising F95, beside rule 34; the code follows (`homeless_basis` reads objections and absence statements as valid only; `cosigned` and `acknowledged` consult the act's status, not only its binding), with three tests (an objection disowned and witnessed; a cosignature disowned; a witness act disowned), and scenario 5 gains step 7e. For the freeze-ready draft and the next core pass.

## F296. A text act carrying `objects` is invalid (14 October 2026, step 15, Text Q2)

*From Fable's Text review (T2).* Nothing said whether a text act may carry `objects`; one naming an agreement's head could read, by Envelopes rule 15 taken literally, as a fork of that agreement (no verdict changes: the Agreements library reads only Agreements acts). Options: (a) allowed and read by no core rule, leaving a later format cMIP free to chain text acts in one act per step (Fable's lean); (b) invalid, a closed shape like `acks` (the project lead's lean, with F295). Explored at the author's request: what (b) gives up is a chain of plain text acts readable by every client (a co-written document, a collective's log, a thread in one agreed order); done as an xtype, a client without the cMIP would show those acts unknown and lose the words; but a cMIP keeps every word readable by leaving each version a plain text act and carrying the chain in a companion act of its own that names it, at one extra act per step. What (a) opens: anyone could attach text acts to another's chain (a deal, a collective's record) by naming it, for a careless client to show as if they belonged there; (b) closes that. No client writes `objects` on a text act today (the connector refuses it: "A text act names no objects"). **Nobody, allegedly: "B."** Text's section 2 becomes a rule (with T2's list of fields: outside `signer`, `binding`, its content key if public, `to` where addressed or answering, F276; inside `spec`, `type` 0, the everyday fields, the payload, optional `refs` and `hint`); a text act carrying `acks` or `objects` is invalid. Chained texts are a cMIP's, by a companion act. For the freeze-ready draft and the core pass with F295.

## F295. An act outside its specification's format, or of a type it does not define, is invalid (14 October 2026, step 15, Text Q1)

*From Fable's Text review (`docs/reviews/fable-step-15-text.md`, T1).* The core library never decoded a text act's payload, so a text act with no text, a non-text field 0, an extra field or an undefined type number was valid to the core verifier, while the barebone client said "not a text act"; Identity already treats its own malformed acts as invalid, and Money's decoder refuses them. Options: (a) invalid, said once in the MICS for every MIR and cMIP; (b) valid but not read. Fable and the project lead leaned (a): one rule for every layer; the unknown verdict keeps the future open for a client that does not implement a specification; and since nothing in MOR is updated, a later format is a new specification with a new hash, shown unknown by older clients, never invalid, so strictness costs no flexibility. **Nobody, allegedly: "Agreed."** The MICS's common conventions gain: an act whose type its specification does not define, or whose payload is not in the format that specification gives for the type, is invalid; a verifier that does not implement the specification shows it as unknown (Envelopes rule 30). The core library decodes the text act's payload strictly where it knows the Text hash (a closed map, field 0 a text string, field 1 a 32-byte hash), with a test. For the freeze-ready draft and the next core pass.

## F294. The relay commitment (type 2) leaves the core for the relay transport cMIP (14 October 2026, step 15, Envelopes Q3)

*From Fable's Envelopes review (E6).* Rule 28's commitment, a relay's public act holding a Merkle root over the ids it holds and their number, had no format and an open tree; nothing in the core library or the clients reads or writes one, nothing in the core relies on it, and the suite's "every mirror matches the commitment" rests on the voting cMIP's own commitment. Options: (a) give it a format and a tree (RFC 9162 over the sorted ids, as the home log); (b) move it to the relay transport cMIP. Fable and the project lead leaned (b) by the admission test: relays are untrusted in the core, and what the core guarantees about storage it guarantees through homes' receipts and logs. **Nobody, allegedly: "That's close [to] the conversation we had yesterday or today. B."** (The relay transport as "relay market business", 13 October.) Rule 28 and type 2 leave the Envelopes MIR; the number is retired, not reused; the relay transport cMIP may define a relay's statement of its store. The suite's "Relays and commitments" line is reread with the freeze-ready draft. Withdrawal's format (E6's other half) is a mechanic: `withdrawal = {}`, its `objects` entry `[publication, publication]`, as the barebone client already writes it.

## F293. The outside of a sealed container is unauthenticated by design; the one-time signature is dropped (14 October 2026, step 15, Envelopes Q2)

*From Fable's Envelopes review (E3).* The text said the container's one-time signature "keeps a relay from re-addressing the container". It does not: the one-time key belongs to nobody, so a relay can drop a recipient and its capsule, sign the rest with a key of its own, and the remaining recipients open it as before (pinned by `core/tests/envelopes.rs`, `a_recipient_can_be_stripped_and_the_container_resigned`). Adding a recipient, opening the act or swapping capsules stays impossible. Fable's options: (a) keep it and reword; (b) drop it, `sealed = [to, locked-act]`; (c) write the one-time key into the locked contents. Fable leaned (a); the project lead (b). The author: "Why does it feel none of the three are the solution and the problem could be another…" **The problem, named in the debate:** the outside of a container that hides its sender cannot be authenticated at all: signed by the sender, it reveals the sender; signed by nobody in particular, it proves nothing. And nothing on the outside needs it: who wrote, to whom (the inner act's own `to`) and what are proven inside, signed by the sender; what a relay can do to the outside, leave a recipient out, it can do by withholding, and delivery is relay market business. **Nobody, allegedly: "I think it does. Let's see if this [bites] us back in the future, b."** The Envelopes MIR states the principle: the outside of a sealed container is unauthenticated by design; all proof lives in the inner act; delivery is the relay market's. The one-time key and signature are dropped from the container's format (`sealed = [to, locked-act]`, capsules as before). A format change before the freeze: the core library, its vectors, the relay's handling and the clients follow, with the freeze-ready draft. *To watch, at the author's word:* whether anything later needs the outside bound; if so, it comes back as a cMIP's, never as a guard on the core's container. E10 (the relay's `MOR/sealed` tag) is reread then: the core no longer uses the tag for a signature.

## F292. Position and summary are judged when a line is rebuilt, not act by act (14 October 2026, step 15, Envelopes Q1)

*From Fable's Envelopes review (`docs/reviews/fable-step-15-envelopes.md`, E2).* Rule 13 says an everyday act MUST carry a position and a running summary consistent with its predecessor, and gives no verdict when it does not. The code reads them only when a rotation's kept line is rebuilt. Options put: (a) invalid wherever a verifier holds the predecessor (a verdict that changes with what was fetched); (b) as the code does: judged only when a line is rebuilt, where an act that does not fit cannot be proven kept; (c), raised in the debate, (b) plus a client-conformance duty to show the act as inconsistent and warn the owner, as a routes fork is shown contested. The author leaned (a) and asked for the debate; the project lead's lean moved to (c), then back to (b) by the admission test: a routes fork changes which routes count, a bad page changes nothing that counts, and the rotation is the remedy under every option, so a warning is good client behaviour, not a core guarantee. A bad page comes only from a buggy client or a careless thief; honest use (parallel devices, a restored backup) never makes one. **Nobody, allegedly: "Buggy apps get caught eventually. B."** Rule 13 gains Fable's sentence: position and summary are not checked act by act; they are checked when a line is rebuilt (Identity, kept ancestry), and an act whose position or summary does not fit the line leading to a kept tip cannot be proven kept. No client-conformance warning. For the freeze-ready draft.

## F291. The MICS document and a test suite are specification kinds of their own (14 October 2026, afternoon)

*Found by the rehearsal of step 17 (`docs/step-17-rehearsal.md`, "What step 17 as written gets wrong", 3).* Step 17 publishes the MICS document and the freeze test suite as specifications (F290), but Development's specification format had six kinds (cMIP, Module, unit, media type, MIR, signature scheme) and neither fits. Options put: (a) publish them under the MIR kind; (b) two new kinds, "the MICS document" and "a test suite", a change to Development's format before the freeze; (c) as long-form documents, with no spec hash and not in the listing. The project lead's lean: (b), since the freeze is the last moment the format can change, the MICS is the map, not a MIR (F269), and MIPs towards MOR 2 will come with suites of their own. **Nobody, allegedly: "Agreed."** Two kinds are added to Development's specification format: the MICS document, and a test suite; published, like the MIRs, with no creator, so their hashes are fixed at the freeze. To build with the rehearsal's other findings.

**Pull request 50 merged.** 1,083 tests, 1,082 passing; the one failure (Dub's `test/web-build.test.ts`, "the release in built/") failed on main the same way before the branch, because the display client was released (`81a34b0`) without a new release of Dub's web client; the fix is that release, run after this merge. Built: the report page asks for a report from the reporter's own identity, and Dub gains **To an identity** (F287); the GitHub mirror both ways (an issue re-published on MOR by the reporting identity after the author's look at the dry run); the release manifest, draft 3, with **implements**; the listing's xtype use counts; **F290** in Development draft 9, with MICS v27 and freeze suite v27. No flaw in a MIR found. *Its question (presentation, the author's desk), taken by the project lead under the working practice, the author may overrule:* the desk's switch **On** reads "Claude may prepare drafts for this identity" and the mirror's notes show under "Claude's note to you", though the mirror is not Claude. Every draft is still signed only on his approval, so nothing is signed without him; but the label is not exact. **As built until step 17; with the reporting identity's making at step 17, the desk gains a second switch, "The GitHub mirror may prepare drafts", and heads each note by who prepared it.** A label that says less than the truth is not left standing past the release.

*Approved by Nobody, allegedly, once the mechanics were explained (the mirror is a plain program, no AI: it copies an issue's words exactly into a draft for the desk): "Understood and approved."* His reading, worth keeping for the second switch's words: "The prepare draft got me thinking AI was involved." So the desk's switch and note heading for the mirror say plainly that it is a program copying from GitHub, not a model.

**Pull requests 45 to 49 merged together** after one fix: the site's tests failed in the combination because two branches each added a page and edited the same count line, which Git merged as one (`docs/bug-reports-on-mor.md`, "The five branches together"); fixed in the test, passing five times in a row and in a full run. The display client released after. **F285** built (an offer under a deal selling a vow counts, stake or not). **The path to MOR 2, draft 1** (`docs/companions/MOR-the-path-to-mor-2-draft-1.md`), for the author's redraft with F289 and the answers recorded above. **The first wave's candidates, draft 1** (`docs/outreach/first-wave-candidates-draft-1.md`), for his approval. **The listing** (website cMIP draft 9, `docs/dubsar.org/listing.html`), tracking extensions first (F288), its twelve readings accepted by the project lead save the two answered above (F290; "implements"). **Bug reports on MOR** (`docs/dubsar.org/report.html`, `clients/reports`, `mor-reports`), the mirror to GitHub; its question 2 (what is mirrored) taken by the project lead: the author looks at each dry run, mirroring only what was answered or picked if it is ever abused. *The report page's question 1* (the box starts on Private; let a page choose?): **Nobody, allegedly: "Bug reports come from people truly on MOR, their own ID."** The report page carries no one-time writing box: a report on MOR is signed by its reporter's own identity, sent from their own client (Dub, its web client for testing, or any other) to the reporting identity; the page says how, shows the answers beside the public reports, and points those not on MOR to the GitHub mirror (F287, both ways). ## Working principle: make the fair route easy (Nobody, allegedly, 14 October 2026)

On how the author of a relay program is found and paid (asked by Nobody, allegedly; laid out by the project lead: found through a release that states what it implements; paid as a work, as overhead in the operator's envelope, or through a dependency pool, case study 19; never forced): **"Cracked software has always existed. As long as MOR makes the fair route easy."** MOR does not police use; it makes paying the people a thing stands on the easiest, most visible route.

## Working practice: development-layer mechanics are not put to the author (Nobody, allegedly, 13 October 2026)

On a question about how a cMIP field links code to its specification: "Don't really need to run these by me." Where a question is the development layer's (how a cMIP or Module does its job, its fields and formats) and the decided rules already bound it, the project lead takes it under the delegation, records it as his, and reports it; it is put to the author only where it touches the core, a stated cost, client conformance or presentation choices he owns.

## Working practice: every question names its area (Nobody, allegedly, 12 October 2026)

"From now on when asking questions or debating inform me of the area: core business, development layer business (modules and cMIP), presentation (site, companion documents…)." Each question put to Nobody, allegedly, opens with its area.

## Working rule: rare cases and markets (Nobody, allegedly, F63)

Costs that fall only on rare cases mostly super users will care about are acceptable. MOR expects markets to kick in where there is value to be protected.

## F53. A receipt counts only alongside the valid act it names (round 2, C2)

**Pattern:** 1.

**Found in review round 2 (C2):** the receipt checks in Identity draft 6 verify the operator's act, the home set, the audit proof and the kept ancestry, but never the identity-chain act the receipt names. Anyone holding a home operator's everyday key, or a dishonest home, could sign a second receipt at any past chain position naming a made-up act, create a "conflict", and un-count a rotation everyone had relied on; every act bound to that rotation then failed everyday check 2 and became invalid, not disputed. A home's everyday key was as powerful as the safety key of every identity relying on that home.

**Checked against the draft (Fable):** holds as written. The report's fix needed two extra conditions, found while drafting: the named act's identity and position must match the receipt's, otherwise a stolen key could receipt a real position-2 rotation "at position 3" and still manufacture a conflict.

**Decided (Nobody, allegedly):** accepted. "Not perfect, but pretty good."

**Resolution (Fable, accepted):** a new first receipt check in Identity:

*A receipt counts, or conflicts with another, only if the verifier holds the identity-chain act it names, that act passes the checks for its type (genesis or rotation), its signer or identity hash is the identity the receipt names, and its position is the position the receipt states (genesis is position 0). A receipt naming an act the verifier does not hold, or one that fails those checks, is neither support nor conflict.*

**Consequences:**
- A conflict now needs two valid rotations at one position, which needs the safety key. A stolen operator key alone can no longer touch any identity's chain.
- A home is proven dishonest only by receipting two genuine rotations: rule 11 broken and provable.
- Verifiers need only the identity chain they already fetch; two honest verifiers holding the same chain agree.
- Tests: exit unchanged; legibility improved (every conflict shows a real act); nothing moves to a cMIP; no conflict with other round 2 fixes; C1 and I3 shrink.

**Residual, on record, not solved here:** a genuine conflict (a thief with the safety key plus a home that receipts both rotations) still un-counts retroactively. Belongs with finality (C1, I5). *Partly resolved by F55: the verdict no longer reaches earlier positions.*

**Core changes:** Identity (Verification procedures: Receipt, new check before the current ones; Conflicting receipts and rule 10b read "two receipts that count"; Definitions: Receipt; Reasoning: "Receipts make homes answerable").

**Freeze suite changes:** scenario 5.7b rewritten: a thief with a stolen operator key signs a receipt naming a made-up act; it names no valid act and changes nothing; the identity is never contested. New 5.7d: the same stolen key receipts a thief's genuine rotation (the thief holds the safety key); the identity is contested at that position until the operator rotates; the audit settles which receipt is real; the journalist's earlier receipt under a cosigned summary survives. Pass criterion "no identity is frozen by a stolen home key" adds "nor contested by a forged receipt".

## F54. Signature schemes are named by number or by specification hash (round 2, C1, first move)

**Pattern:** 3, in the good-ancestor sense: the format `signing-key = [scheme: 1, key: bstr .size 32]` protects determinism today and traps every owner after an elliptic-curve break, since no rotation can install a post-quantum everyday key. The MOR 2 case study promised what the format forbade.

**Found in review round 2 (C1):** the way out after a break rests on schnorr-signed acts, and the everyday key can never leave schnorr.

**Options weighed:** reserved scheme numbers (the report's suggestion; a registry in the frozen text) or a specification hash naming a signature-scheme specification, like units (F34).

**Decided (Nobody, allegedly):** specification hash.

**Resolution (Fable, accepted):**
- `scheme = 1 / 2 / 3 / hash`: the founding numbers as now, or the hash of a signature-scheme specification (Production) that defines key encoding, signature encoding and verification. `key: bstr`, no size bound.
- The same form applies to the safety-key commitment, so that SLH-DSA itself can be replaced one day; the commitment already hashes `scheme || key`.
- A rotation's own validity never depends on the kind of everyday key it installs, so every client verifies the rotation; acts signed under a scheme a client lacks are unknown, never valid (F11, fail closed). Migration is by adoption.
- Signature-scheme specifications are a kind of specification in Production; how a client learns one is the same as for units.

**Not changed by this:** an identity that has not migrated its everyday key before a break is not saved by it. Case study 10 must say migration comes first (reaction).

**Core changes:** Identity (Encoding: signature schemes table gains the hash form; Shared parts: `signing-key`, `safety-commit`; Every act check 4 "scheme is known" reads "known to this client, otherwise unknown"); Envelope (signature field); Production (signature scheme as a kind of specification, alongside units); case study 10.

**Freeze suite changes:** scenario 8 adds: a signature-scheme specification is published; an identity rotates to an everyday key of that scheme; a client without it shows the identity's later acts as unknown and the rotation as valid; a client with it verifies them.

## F55. A dishonesty verdict never reaches backwards (round 2, C1, second move)

**Pattern:** 1 (a verdict producible by the home's everyday key alone, or by anyone after a break) and 4 ("from then on" was undefined).

**Found in review round 2 (C1):** two non-extending log summaries were "proof the home is dishonest" with no stated consequence, and a dishonest home's receipts counted for nothing "from then on" with no definition of "then". Read together, a stolen operator key today, or anyone after a break, could strip every receipt a home ever signed and un-count years of rotations for every identity there.

**Decided (Nobody, allegedly):** both rules, once shown to rest on different reasons: rule 1 closes C1; rule 2 makes an undefined word decidable and keeps "nothing relied on is silently erased" against a thief holding the safety key and a bad home, who may contest the future but not erase the past.

**Resolution (Fable, accepted):**
1. *Two non-extending summaries carry no verdict on receipts.* Auditors MUST NOT cosign further summaries of that home; clients SHOULD flag the home to its owners. Receipts under a cosigned summary stay protected (F45); the rest are judged as before.
2. *Dishonesty is position-bound.* A home proven dishonest for an identity at chain position n (two counting receipts for two genuine rotations at n, F53) counts for nothing at positions after n. Its receipts at positions before n keep counting. The chain position is the "then"; no clock.

**After F53, F54 and F55, post-break:** an owner holding both keys still leaves; an owner holding only the safety key can be blocked by a forged objection on a homeless rotation. Inherent; case study 10 says migration comes first.

**Core changes:** Identity (Log summaries and audits rule 1: consequence stated; Conflicting receipts rule 3 and rule 10d: "at positions after n"; Reasoning: "Homes audit each other", "Contested, not frozen").

**Freeze suite changes:** 5.7d (F53) adds: the home is proven dishonest at that position; the journalist's earlier rotations keep counting; a pair of forged non-extending summaries from the stolen key changes no receipt's standing, and the auditors stop cosigning.

## F56. A home closes by a rotation of its operator (round 2, I3)

**Pattern:** 1. "This home is gone", the fact that unlocks a homeless rotation no home can object to, was signed by the operator's everyday key. Side effect: the closure invalidated every later act in the operator's sequences, so an honest operator who closed gracefully lost their own identity.

**Found in review round 2 (I3):** a thief with a stolen operator key could close the home, making every identity there provably homeless; with a victim's safety key as well, the thief walked that identity out uncontested.

**Options weighed:** keep closure as an everyday act, narrow its effect, and make a homeless rotation resting on it provisional (the report); or declare closure in a rotation of the operator (Fable).

**Decided (Nobody, allegedly):** closure by rotation. "Closure is a rare, force-majeure case; a forced rotation is warranted."

**Resolution (Fable, accepted):**
- Act type 11 (home closure) is removed. The rotation payload gains a closure flag: the signer's home stops serving every identity.
- For the homeless procedure, a home is gone (step 4, first case) when a rotation of its operator that counts declares closure. Rule 34 reads: a home that closes SHOULD do so by rotation.
- The operator's identity continues under its new key; only the home role ends. No sequence is invalidated.
- Follows the F10 principle: anything whose theft causes lasting harm is changed only with the safety key.

**Costs stated:** a graceful closure spends a safety key; an operator who has lost their safety key cannot close gracefully, and their identities fall back on auditors' absence statements or the verifier's own attempt, as before.

**Touches I5:** a closure resting on a counting rotation is as settled as that rotation, which gives the closure case of finality a clean footing.

**Core changes:** Identity (Types table; rotation payload field 12; Home closure, objection and absence statement procedure; Homeless rotation step 4; rules 11a, 34; Reasoning: "Nobody dies with their home").

**Freeze suite changes:** 5.7c: the vanished home is replaced by a home whose operator closes by rotation; the owner re-homes; a thief with a stolen operator everyday key cannot close the home, and the home still objects to the thief's homeless rotation.

## F57. A disowned act is void unless relied on, like an act outside the kept ancestry (round 2, I4)

**Pattern:** 3. Rule 16 protected continuity (a disowned act sits inside the owner's own kept line) and trapped the owner whose device kept appending after a thief's act: keeping later genuine acts meant keeping the thief's, and disowning left it valid.

**Found in review round 2 (I4):** a thief's stake transfer inside the kept line could only be marked disputed; the accomplice held a valid stake, and the owner's only alternative was voiding their own later acts.

**Decided (Nobody, allegedly):** accepted.

**Resolution (Fable, accepted):** rules 16 and 17 collapse into one: *an act outside the kept ancestry, or within it but listed as disowned, is void, unless another identity acknowledged it (Envelope) or a named keeper recorded it before recording the rotation (Law); then it is shown as disputed.* Later acts in the line stay valid: their validity rests on being inside the kept ancestry, not on the disowned act's validity; hash links and running summaries are untouched.

**Why nothing new becomes escapable:** an owner could always void an unacknowledged act by rotating to a tip before it; this removes only the price of losing later genuine acts. The protection against escaping deals was never rule 16 but acknowledgement and keepers (scenarios 1.5b, 2.5c), which still apply: a disowned sale the buyer acknowledged or a keeper recorded is a visible dispute, never nothing (F47).

**Core changes:** Identity (rules 16 and 17 merged; Reasoning: "Nothing relied on is silently erased"); Law (one line: a void or disputed act on an agreement chain confers nothing; to be written with I20).

**Freeze suite changes:** scenario 1 step 5: the thief's stake transfer was appended to before the contributor noticed; the contributor keeps the later genuine acts and disowns the transfer; unacknowledged and unrecorded, it is void, and the accomplice's completing act confers no stake. Component "Disowning an act within the kept ancestry" reads "void unless relied on, then disputed".

## F58. A keeper record counts only alongside the valid act it names (round 2, challenge 4)

**Pattern:** 1. A keeper record is one of the two things that turn a void act into a dispute after a rotation (F57). Keepers are sealed (F37), so nothing required them to hold the act they record; a thief could buy "dispute" status for any 32 bytes.

**Explored:** a keeper duty "MUST NOT record an act it does not hold" was proposed and withdrawn on the author's question ("if unverifiable, what is the value?"): it is keeper hygiene, invisible to verifiers, pattern 4. The verifiable half carries all the value: a record of bytes nobody can produce as a valid act changes no verdict, and a record of a real act signed with a stolen key adds nothing beyond what an accomplice's acknowledgement already does (M4, accepted).

**Decided (Nobody, allegedly):** accepted, verifiable half only.

**Resolution (Fable, accepted):** in Law, keepers: *a keeper record counts, for the purpose of the disowned-or-outside rule, only if the verifier holds the act it names, that act is valid as an act, and the keeper is one named by an agreement the act's `objects` names.* Keepers stay sealed: they hold the locked act and check its outside, which a sealed notary can do.

**Not touched:** M24, a keeper delaying its record of the rotation, remains accepted trust in keepers, to be stated in Law's reasoning.

**Core changes:** Law (Keepers: the counting condition; Reasoning: what a record proves, and the delay trust).

**Freeze suite changes:** scenario 1 step 5 adds: a keeper record naming an act nobody holds confers no dispute status.

## F59. The furthest-along default is replaced by the majority rule as default (round 2, I6)

**Pattern:** 4 (a procedure two honest verifiers could run differently: look-ahead depth and branch-dependent home sets were unspecified), with a known-unsafe outcome by its own text.

**Found in review round 2 (I6):** with several homes and no declared rule, the furthest-along default was underspecified. F8 had kept it so that a genesis with several homes and no rule still resolves.

**Explored:** requiring a rule whenever several homes are declared (Fable, the reaction). Nobody, allegedly: a single home must stay possible at genesis, since forcing several homes is too much friction for a new user; multiple options are fine with one as the forced default.

**Decided (Nobody, allegedly):** one option is forced if none is selected. Recorded as a working rule above.

**Resolution (Fable, accepted):**
- One home: no rule, as now.
- Several homes and no declared rule: the majority rule applies, with a threshold greater than half the distinct operators, computed from the home list. An authoritative home needs an index the owner did not give, so majority is the default.
- The furthest-along procedure, rule 20 and its branch in "Which rotation counts" step 3 are removed; the homeless procedure's "every home under the default" case goes with it.
- Cost stated: an owner who added backups as mere copies gets a safety network by default, stricter, and may declare an authoritative home at any rotation.
- *Amended by F62: when a self-hosted home is declared, the default is instead that the self-hosted home is authoritative.*

**Core changes:** Identity (genesis payload comment for field 3; rule 5; rule 20; Which rotation counts step 3; Homeless rotation step 4; Reasoning: "Two forms of home rule"); core v11 (home rules).

**Freeze suite changes:** component "Home rules" drops "furthest-along default" and adds "majority by default when several homes declare no rule". 5.6 adds: a fourth identity with three homes and no declared rule resolves by majority.

## F60. A rotation's receipts are judged under the audit requirement it declares (round 2, I1)

**Pattern:** 3, the exact case: a protection the owner chose (audited receipts from named auditors) could not be removed by the safety key alone, because the removing rotation needed receipts audited by the auditors who were gone.

**Found in review round 2 (I1):** auditors close or stop cosigning; no receipt counts; no rotation counts, including the one that would drop the requirement. Step 4 of the finding also left open which requirement judges the new-home receipts of a homeless rotation or escape.

**Decided (Nobody, allegedly):** accepted.

**Resolution (Fable, accepted):** *receipts for a rotation are judged under the audit requirement that rotation itself declares, or, if it declares none, under the requirement in effect before it.* The same sentence covers homeless rotations and escapes: their new-home receipts are judged under what the rotation declares or inherits. Consistent with rotation check 5, which already judges a new home rule against the new home set.

**What it opens and does not:** a safety-key thief can rotate with "no audit" past the auditors; but audit never protected against a safety-key thief, only against stolen home keys and history erasure (F15, F45), and the thief still needs the home to receipt the rotation (rule 11, first held wins, device policies). No guarantee lost; a trap removed.

**Core changes:** Identity (Receipt check 3: "the audit requirement the named rotation declares, or the one in effect before it"; Homeless rotation step 5; rule 10e).

**Freeze suite changes:** 5.7c adds: the journalist's declared auditor closes; the journalist rotates, dropping the requirement; the rotation counts on the home's receipt alone.

## F61. A device policy at a single or authoritative home is stated as a risk, not fixed (round 2, I2)

**Pattern:** 3 in appearance, 4 underneath: a refusal is the absence of a receipt, which nothing proves, so no rule can distinguish "the home refused" from "the home never saw it".

**Found in review round 2 (I2):** a lost phone at a home with a device policy closes every door: normal rotation refused (rule 12), homeless rotation objected to (rule 11a), escape needs the lost signing key.

**Explored and withdrawn:** "a home that refuses must not object" (the report's first direction). Unverifiable, and it would weaken the objection, the one thing that stops a safety-key thief walking past careful homes (rule 32). The reaction reached the same conclusion.

**Decided (Nobody, allegedly):** accepted as a plain statement plus client conformance.

**Resolution (Fable, accepted):**
- Rule 12 gains: *a home's acceptance conditions can refuse the owner as well as a thief; where the condition depends on a device the owner may lose, losing that device at a single or authoritative home can mean losing the identity.*
- Client conformance: *a client MUST NOT choose a device-bound policy at a single or authoritative home by default; where the owner chooses one, the client MUST say what it costs.*
- The escape already carries the answer for an owner who prepared: it needs the current signing key, which is a seed that can be backed up apart from the phone. Rule 37 adds "and backed up"; clients and key Modules offering device-bound policies SHOULD require that backup first.

No verdict changes; two verifiers are unaffected.

**Core changes:** Identity (rules 12, 37; client conformance under 18a or a new 12a; Reasoning: "Both keys always get out").

**Freeze suite changes:** 5.7c adds: the journalist's phone is lost at a home with a device policy; the signing seed was backed up; the journalist leaves with both keys.

## F62. A self-hosted identity is trusted on its own signatures; other homes are backups (round 2, C3)

**Pattern:** 3, literally: the most sovereign setup could never rotate, since its receipt had to be signed by a key that exists only once the rotation counts.

**Found in review round 2 (C3):** the circularity, and two honest verifiers reading "trusted for its own chain" differently.

**Question asked first (brainstorm notes):** is self-hosting a home like any other, or a different trust model? Reading A (home like any other) needs receipts and therefore declared auditors, which makes the self-hoster depend on others. Reading B: the identity has chosen to be trusted on its own signatures.

**Decided (Nobody, allegedly):** reading B. "In this event other homes are assumed to be used as backup, not safety network."

**Resolution (Fable, accepted):**
- *For a self-hosted home, the rotation the identity itself serves at that position counts, with no receipt and no first-held-wins.* Two rotations at one position served by a self-host make the position contested, as with conflicting receipts, until the owner serves one.
- A thief holding the safety key wins at once, as at a single lax home. The text says so; the risk is the owner's choice.
- When a self-hosted home is declared alongside others and no home rule is given, the default is that the self-hosted home is authoritative and the others are backups (working rule of F59: one of the declared options is the default). The owner MAY still declare a threshold explicitly, in which case the self-host counts as one operator whose served rotation stands in for a receipt.
- The homeless procedure does not treat a self-host as "gone": the owner is the home, and rotates to new homes by serving the rotation from anywhere.

**Checks:** exit improves; nothing hidden; adoption decides; mechanical from the served chain; no conflict with F53 (a self-host's rotation is held by definition).

**Core changes:** Identity (Shared parts: null operator comment; Resolving an operator; Which rotation counts step 3; rule 5 and the F59 default; Reasoning: new entry "Self-hosting is a trust model"); core v11 (homes).

**Freeze suite changes:** new component "Self-hosted identity: rotation counts on its own signatures; other homes as backups". Scenario 5 adds a self-hosted identity of the journalist that rotates with no receipt, and a second scenario where a thief with its safety key wins at once, shown as the stated cost.

## F63. A homeless rotation is final once the next rotation counts (round 2, I5)

**Pattern:** 2. Identity, the ground layer, made finality depend on an anchor defined in Law, which Identity-only clients cannot compute; and the closure case had no finality at all.

**Found in review round 2 (I5):** two clients, same data, different answers on whether a homeless rotation could still be overturned; a graceful closure left the owner permanently provisional.

**Options weighed:** define the anchor check inside Identity (still an upward dependency in substance); "final after n later receipts" (needs a number the core would have to pick); "final once the identity's next rotation counts" (the reaction's direction, Fable's recommendation).

**Decided (Nobody, allegedly):** next rotation. Cost accepted: "rare cases mostly super users will care about; MOR expects markets to kick in where there is value to be protected." Recorded as a working rule above.

**Resolution (Fable, accepted):**
- *A homeless rotation at position n is final once a rotation at position n plus one counts under the home rule the homeless rotation declared.* Nobody can name an act before it exists, so any objection or receipt surfacing afterwards is late by construction. No clock, no anchor, no Law.
- Applies alike to a homeless rotation resting on a closure by rotation (F56) or on auditors' absence statements. One resting only on the verifier's own failed attempt stays provisional, shown as "re-homed without audit", as before.
- Anchoring remains Law's tool for Law's purposes; Identity no longer depends on it.

**Core changes:** Identity (Homeless rotation: Finality rewritten; rule 33 area; Reasoning: "Nobody dies with their home"); Law (anchoring task: drop the reference to Identity finality).

**Freeze suite changes:** component "Homeless rotation ... final once audited and anchored" reads "final once the next rotation counts". 5.7c: the journalist rotates once more after re-homing; a late objection changes nothing.

## F64. Double entry: a payer's claim is evidence on equal footing with a receipt (round 2, C5, I11, I13, challenge 1)

**Pattern:** 1, in its purest money form: the only record of money arriving was written by whoever it arrived at. The same shape appeared four times (C5, I11, I13, I18); one rule covers three, and I18 gets its own sentence in Law because the pair of parties differs.

**Found in review round 2 (C5):** a receiver that delivers the key but signs no receipt, or a smaller one, leaves co-owners with no core-level view of what arrived; Finance rule 10 allowed a payer's claim only when the payee "signed nothing". Checked against Finance draft 4: holds; the claim format already carries rail proof, payee, amount and what was followed.

**Decided (Nobody, allegedly):** accepted. "One stone, multiple birds."

**Resolution (Fable, accepted):** Finance rule 10 is replaced by:

*A payment claim is a payer's record of a payment, carrying the rail's proof. It is admitted as evidence on equal footing with a receipt. A payer MAY publish one at any time; it is private by default, like every act, so publishing it reveals the payer only to whom the payer chooses. Where a claim and a receipt name the same rail proof and disagree in amount, payee, or what the payment fulfils, the disagreement is shown as an open question on the receiver, and the greater amount counts as received until the receiver signs a receipt matching the proof.*

**What it answers:** C5 (a payer can put an unreceipted payment on record; owners' clients count it against the split service); I11 (under-reporting and misattribution are visible mismatches); I13 (the payer's claim fixes what arrived, so with a maximum fee per payout stated in the split plan, a shortfall beyond it is computable and an open obligation of the service); challenge 1 (payer-signed records as equal evidence, private by default).

**The measured promise (reaction, adopted):** the core cannot make every payment legible; a seller can deliver outside MOR and a buyer can stay silent. *Hiding income requires the payer's silence or collusion.* The core principle "legible greed" is reworded to that. Payer-side splitting cMIPs, where each owner is paid directly, remain the models that need no service's honesty; "Who can earn" says so.

**Checks:** mechanical, from a claim and a receipt sharing one rail proof; privacy principle kept (the payer publishes by choice); no conflict with F46 or the vault rule.

**Core changes:** Finance (Payment claim definition; rule 10; a line under Receivers and senders); Law (split plan: maximum fee per payout; rule 24a; rule 29 counts claims as well as receipts); Core principle "legible greed"; "Who can earn" (Hidden fees; split models).
